in writing. i welcome the fact that the european council has reached a compromise on the european union's economic agenda for 2020 and on helping greece. the economic growth targeted is also a precondition for overcoming the labour market and social crisis. it is good to see that the european council is paying close attention to the low-skilled as well as the young and the elderly in order to increase employment. increasing employment for disadvantaged groups should be accompanied by the acquisition of skills with market value and the promotion of lifelong learning. i also agree with the hungarian government's desire to think in terms of quantified territorial cohesion rather than poverty-related objectives in the fight against poverty, since those living in poverty and especially extreme poverty live in regions within the union that are well defined on a territorial basis. this would also make it possible to coordinate the management of the complex problem of poverty and of regions lagging behind. it is also important that, when committing to the 2020 targets, policy-makers have accurate data on the social level and proportion of poverty. while the management of the economic crisis and the eu 2020 roadmap set the course to be followed, the visegrad countries understandably ask that the debate on this does not determine the long-term financial perspective after 2013. the discussion of this will require longer negotiations and it is not possible to predict the financial room for manoeuvre that the eu will have in 2012-13.
in writing. the final document of the march council meeting very rightly addresses the issue of climate change in particular. this is also welcome, because the failure of the copenhagen summit has created a very uncertain situation in climate policy. flexibility mechanisms, including in particular quota trading schemes, are likely to remain important tools for the eu's climate action efforts. however, their functioning is still controversial today. for example, last year, a large amount of tax fraud was committed in the united kingdom in connection with a quota sale. more recently, allowances sold by the hungarian government were found to have been illegally returned to the eu ets. the emission rights were intended to be used twice, in practice through offshore companies. these are just two examples of how the emissions trading system is not working smoothly. abuses particularly disadvantage those actors making real efforts to mitigate environmental damage. the legal loopholes in the quota trading system should be addressed as soon as possible, taking lessons from bad examples. the european commission must act against the use of licences without real performance, in bad faith or not according to their intended purpose. in addition, full legal coherence between international and union climate protection regulation is needed. to this end, it is also appropriate to strengthen union surveillance procedures. flexibility mechanisms are still needed for effective climate regulation. however, flexibility must not be unpredictable and must not create opportunities for abuse.
the rapporteur. - madam president, mr sefcovic, vice-president of the commission, i would like to thank you for your kind words. many speakers in parliament begin their speeches by saying that their topic is important, very important or perhaps one of the most important topics. i would like to say the same thing, but on a much more substantial basis than many people do, because the subject we are discussing, the delegation of powers by parliament, the delegation of powers by the commission, is perhaps one of the most important, but rarely mentioned, innovations of the treaty of lisbon. those who criticized the lisbon treaty spoke of a lack of democracy. ladies and gentlemen, i would like to say that this amendment, which guarantees the parliament's right to delegate, is one of the most significant additions to parliament's powers. it means that the representatives directly elected by the citizens can now, through the power of parliament and the legislator, control and veto the executive decisions and decisions taken by the european commission. the fact that the treaty of lisbon, which we talk about a lot in connection with the extension of parliament's powers, has increased the number of codecision procedures considerably, the range of subjects in which parliament can take part has been extended, and the question of parliament's delegation of power has been added to, is not only a quantitative but also a qualitative extension. so from now on, as a result of a long struggle and a long struggle, which our colleagues here in the european parliament have been waging for many years, we have continued to do so, it is guaranteed that the decisions of the european commission, which are not taken in its own capacity but in the context of implementing powers, will be subject to parliamentary scrutiny. of course, many would argue that this control has existed in a formal sense. in a sense, yes, but with deadlines and in circumstances and as a result of interinstitutional cooperation under the law which did not involve real, effective control. i would also like to point out that this new possibility, namely article 290 of the treaty of lisbon, will also transform the work of parliament in the coming years. especially if we, as members of parliament, take this jurisdiction seriously and can really decide on these matters. to make the outside world and those outside the profession understand what this is all about, because it is a very complex subject in technical terms, but it is in fact a democratic institution and it is intended to eliminate the so-called european democratic deficit. it means that the parliament and the council, the two european legislatures, cannot deal with every detail on their own. parliament meets every month, and sometimes there are occasions like this, when we meet for nothing and cannot vote, but parliament still has to check the questions that are put by the council or the commission under delegated powers. from now on, you'll have the opportunity. in my report i have tried to make it clear that parliament is committed to these rights. we will not accept any additional obligations beyond those laid down in the treaty. we do not accept it because we want to exercise our rights to the full and i think that european democracy will expand as a result. this requires close cooperation with the commission and the council, but we must not forget that the legislators, and thus parliament and the council, have the power to do so. i would like to thank all my colleagues for their cooperation on this complex issue and for the fact that in the future parliament will make full use of this new licence.
the rapporteur. − i thank my colleagues for the debate, as this report was produced in very close cooperation with both committees, and i would like to highlight the environment committee, which will have many such tasks in the coming period, and with the political parties. i'll add, my fellow officers, that this is a struggle. fighting the commission, fighting the council, fighting for our rights. this report is about making clear that these are our rights and we stand by them. it is much easier to do this when i have the backing of both committees and colleagues from other parties and when i make it clear to the partner institutions that parliament is firmly behind these issues. however, in order to be able to exercise this right effectively, i take mr sefcovic's words to heart, namely that the general alignment, or general adjustment, could be accelerated in the coming months and that we could really extend these rights as far as possible. we think it's important to focus on areas where co-decision has not been possible before. another very important element that colleagues have mentioned here is the importance of access to information. this should be laid down in an interinstitutional agreement, and negotiations are under way with both the commission and the council, but we would like to know what the council is doing, what kind of consultations it is having on these matters. and as for whether there's been an expansion or a decline in rights, ladies and gentlemen, read the report, look at the legislation. i can't see how this is anything other than an extension of our rights. we have no say in what happens to the licences we've already delegated. parliament was virtually left out of the process. they were heard out of courtesy, but the deadlines were so short that parliament did not even meet during that time, and now we can veto it, we can withdraw it, we don't even have to give our reasons, so it's a clear extension of rights. we are the ones who have the right and we delegate it to the commission, and as a result i cannot interpret this as anything other than an extension of our rights. colleagues, it is now up to us to exercise these rights in the future.
in writing. – i warmly welcome the outcome of the report by mr szájer. defining the new legislative system is a difficult and complex issue. i am convinced that the treaty on the functioning of the european union (tfeu), and in particular articles 290 and 291 thereof, reinforces the democratic character of the union and rationalises its legal order. the introduction of the novel concept of legislative acts has far-reaching consequences. the report emphasizes the slight differences among the key co-legislators, the competences and the detailed agenda of how they work with each other. the real question is: in case the ec gets more legislative competences and wider legislative authorities, which means an immediate increase in community interests, what kind of control methods should be introduced on behalf of the council and the ep? the new legislative framework of the tfeu set up the tools but there has not been any practical experience of their adaptation at community level. finally, i urge the co-legislators to have an interinstitutional agreement as soon as possible between the parliament, the council and the commission on the implementation of article 290, as there is an urgent need for greater transparency.
this is a blue-chip question for the previous speaker. i'd like to ask you a question in half a minute.
the most important aspect of the le foll report is that without agriculture we would have no chance of tackling global climate change, but i am thinking in particular of the role of forests, which today are not sufficiently supported. i totally agree that agriculture needs to be greener, greener for the future. on the one hand, this is essential, on the other hand, agriculture today produces many environmental public goods - biodiversity, landscape protection, water cleanliness and so on - that are not sufficiently paid for by the market, not sufficiently paid for by the common agricultural policy, so in the common agricultural policy after 2013 we have to create a market for environmental public goods so that farmers receive those extra services, compensation for the extra services they provide.
it is very important that, according to the dorfmann report, the cap budget should continue to give priority to supporting agricultural areas with natural handicaps, without which these areas would suffer very serious ecological damage and social problems. it is a good thing that the commission and it is commendable that the commission is entrusting the member states with the task of drawing up a classification system based on the eight criteria. at the same time, i would like to point out that there are still differences between member states, there is discrimination, not only in the area of direct payments, which affects the new member states, particularly the commissioner's country, latvia, but also other new member states. in my country, the supply of water to the sand dunes is a particular problem, as the groundwater level has fallen by 4-5 metres in four decades and has become a semi-desert area, but in southern europe it is also a problem, so it is particularly important to address the issue of water management in the future of the cap.
i congratulate the rapporteur on not only the specific proposals but also on the whole philosophy. it's great because in the future, it wants to give support to those who are actively farming, who are effectively cultivating the land. i also think it would be excellent to eliminate sectoral and other distortions and inequalities in the future common agricultural policy, and here i must again underline the discrimination affecting the new member states. it is also very important that not only community legislation should be simplified, but that much depends on the implementation by the member states. let me give you a typical example: two years ago the council adopted a regulation making it compulsory to have a unique electronic identification system for sheep and goats aged less than one year old for slaughter. this has, in the case of poorer member states, where the state has not been able to support it, essentially brought the sector to the ground and put it in a hopeless situation. this shows that over-regulation, over-bureaucracy can very often cause very serious damage to the common agricultural policy, which is why mr ashworth's report is important, as is mr dorfmann's and mr le foll's.
the report by richard ashworth makes excellent proposals to simplify the cap. it is of great value that you look at the matter from the farmers' point of view. of course, it is also important to use taxpayers' money in the most orderly way possible. but it's a tool, not an end. the aim is to achieve the full range of the policy objectives. agencies often confuse these two, to the detriment of the objective. it requires a radical change of attitude. a culture of cooperation is needed between customers, farmers and officials, rather than a culture of subordination. the commission should also develop legislation to provide for an obligation to compensate and sanction the office where its failure to act harms the interests of farmers. the right of beneficiaries to seek redress before an independent tribunal should also be ensured. we don't have that option in hungary today.
in writing. although the volcanic dust cloud was fortunately not fatal as a result of the action of the aviation authorities, information and coordination were insufficient in europe. the airspace blockade has put hundreds of thousands of europeans in a difficult position, preventing them from reaching their destinations. everyone is trying to deal with the unexpected problem, but the lack of information has made it even more difficult. passengers were often not given adequate information, either by telephone or on the internet. it was also disturbing to see conflicting information from aviation authorities and airlines. there was also a lack of coordination between air and ground traffic. we have seen firsthand the importance of modernising transport links between member states and of building trans-european transport networks. i welcome the commission's decisive crisis management action. the task force should strengthen coordination between aviation and airspace control authorities and should also examine the validity of security measures introduced in the 1980s. i suggest that in a similar crisis, the european parliament should not make the already chaotic traffic more difficult by moving between brussels and strasbourg, but should instead hold its plenary session in brussels.
in writing. thousands of people are stranded in vulnerable conditions at airports due to days of restrictions and flight cancellations following the icelandic volcanic eruption. despite the significant delays (more than 2 hours for flights of less than 1500 km), the airlines did not provide any or only partial assistance and did not provide accurate information for the continuation of the journey to the destination. passengers were not provided with free telephone, fax, e-mail or internet access, or even with transfers and accommodation, to which they were entitled under the eu rules in force. passengers with disabilities, the elderly and families with young children are much more vulnerable in such an emergency situation, as they have been. this unexpected circumstance has shown that passenger rights previously respected can be annulled in a matter of minutes and has shown that the passenger charter, which i had previously advocated, is particularly necessary to prevent a repetition of the inaction of airlines and the competent bodies. however, i propose that it should be mandatory for every passenger to be informed automatically of any exceptional change and to be informed of alternative solutions by sending a message to the mobile phone of the customer concerned.

author. - my colleagues! in recent years, very important decisions have been taken in the european union to protect our environment, such as the decision on biodiversity or the water framework directive. the european union water framework directive obliges member states to protect the quality of our waters and prevent pollution. is that a good goal? yes, that's right. are we obligated to do everything in our power to achieve that goal? obviously, yes. is there any mining process that endangers our waters, our environment? i'm afraid it is. and it's a very, very dangerous and also outdated technology. a number of my colleagues and i would like to see this technology banned throughout the european union. the cyanide disaster in tizi ouzou 10 years ago and the accidents since then have been a warning to us. my fellow officers! this is both a fortunate and an urgent moment. it is fortunate because, according to the information received from the commission today, only three countries are using this cyanide mining process and it is fortunate because there are three other countries which have banned the cyanide process, setting an example for the other eu member states. but it's also a time of great urgency, because the rising price of gold is prompting new mines to be opened across europe with this dangerous and outdated technology. so the danger lurking around us is immense. my fellow officers! if we're serious about protecting our water, we can't put cyanide poison ponds alongside our rivers and lakes, and that's what this outdated technology is doing. if we're serious about protecting biodiversity, we can't afford to use techniques that can kill life in rivers, from microorganisms to crustaceans to fish. it's time, colleagues, for action. we should not wait for another disaster to warn us of this. finally, i would like to thank all the colleagues who are present and who will be taking part in the debate and who were unable to be present because of the eruption of the volcano, for their help in drafting this resolution, since we were able to reach a compromise and table a joint text which enjoys the support not only of the people's party but also of the green group, the socialist group, the liberals and the conservatives, and i think that this is absolutely justified because of the seriousness of the matter. i ask and encourage my colleagues to maintain this support until the end of the decision.

in writing. over the past two years, i have spoken on a number of occasions in the ep plenary and in various forums about the dangers of cyanide mining. i also wrote to the commissioner for the environment, stavros dimas, about the mining projects in romania (verespatak) and bulgaria (chelopech and krumovgrad). the mining of cyanide is also known as the "chemical atom bomb", which is very dangerous for our environment. since 1990, there have been some 30 major cyanide mining accidents worldwide. the tisza disaster ten years ago is europe's worst environmental disaster since chernobyl. in romania, the golden river, which flows into the tisza river, has recently been polluted by a gold mine that closed 40 years ago. last year, when visiting a nearby mine (roșia poieni), president traian băsescu himself declared: "you cannot sit on an ecological bomb like this, it's murder". with regard to the new mining investment plans in romania (verespatak, nagybánya, felsőcsertés, etc.), i would like to stress that the prohibition of cyanide mining is not just a romanian problem, and by no means an ethnic problem, but a universal european problem, on which both individual eu member states and the various parliamentary groups can reach a reasonable agreement. europe cannot remain indifferent to the cyano-catastrophes that have occurred and those that threaten to occur in the future. it is in our common interest to protect people and the environment from cyanide poisoning, just as we do from radioactive radiation and air pollution. i ask that the honourable house vote on our motion.

ensures the post-crisis management and creates the possibility for the union and all its member states to be competitive in the global economic environment, to develop in an environmentally sustainable way and thus to increase the living standards of all eu citizens, even appreciably in the short term. but i would like to make two comments on this new strategy, which i think provides the foundation, but it is important to talk about two things. first, some caution and care should be exercised with regard to quantifiable targets. these should be ambitious, but also realistic and achievable. one that takes account of the different starting points and capacities of each member state. poverty in the uk is different from poverty in bulgaria. our education systems are different. for example, the question arises whether it is worthwhile to increase the proportion of university graduates to 40% everywhere, if this does not increase the number of graduates who are unemployed. and convergence of the union. i agree with the support for the towing industries, which are the engine of the european economy. at the same time, it should not be forgotten that the development gaps within the 27 member states would be further widened by such a unilateral competitiveness policy that does not address the specificities of the member states. without internal cohesion, there is no external competitiveness. closing the gap would mean more markets, more viable demand, more innovation potential and less social concern for the union as a whole. it is therefore necessary to provide the framework for the less-favoured areas to benefit from the benefits of the internal market. so in time, everyone could become competitive on their own. i hope that cohesion policy will find its the strategy for the future of europe is based on two sets of values, and i would like to point out that these two sets of values are very difficult to reconcile, or perhaps not at all, and that a choice must be made between the two. one value system has the keywords competitiveness, growth, globalisation, and the other has the keyword sustainability. we talk about sustainability in the environmental sense. we know that the growth, the force of globalization, is having a tremendous negative impact on the environment. there is no sustainability. we're talking about sustainability in the social sense. people, small farmers, small and medium-sized enterprises, etc., who cannot keep up with the excessive competition and globalization, they collapse, they fall out of competition, they become impoverished, and this is completely contrary to sustainability in the social sense. i think, and my constituents think, that sustainability is the one of the two values.
in writing. mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the house of representatives, in the european union, economic growth is stagnating and unemployment is above 10%. this means developing a new strategy for sustainable growth and job creation that will be able to boost the union. it depends on its ability to renew itself, its ability to put its economy on a new footing and its ability to give eu citizens a new way of living. this renewal in the union can be brought about by innovation, research and development. the european union is developing a growth strategy for 2020 based on innovation, research and development for the future of eu citizens, which can ensure continued economic growth for the region and provide new jobs for its inhabitants. without resources, r&d cannot create the necessary financial backing and thus cannot exploit the innovation opportunities. innovation can only come from a common effort, a common support. the funds come from three sources: the union, the member states and the private sector. in order to bring innovation to the level of the enterprise, adequate human resources are needed. this is achieved through high quality education and training, coordinated at member state level. universities should be supported to train young researchers who will be able to provide continuous innovation opportunities for smes. in the field of education, digitisation and reducing energy consumption are the two main directions that offer innovation opportunities.
in writing. the eu2020 strategy is being put to practical use by the integrated economic and employment guidelines. i also think it is important to say, in relation to the draft employment guidelines, that inclusive growth can only be imagined if investment in people is actually made. longer life does not in itself mean longer productive lives. particularly relevant in hungary is the investment in healthcare, where people die significantly earlier than in western europe, while childbearing rates are also falling. and we need to maintain the right skills in old age, which is only possible with a more modern, more accessible health care. in fact, we should also think about jobs that are accessible to older people, who are increasingly active, and that benefit younger people who are already disabled. health should therefore also be given a special priority (such as working conditions, more successful rehabilitation, improvement of health protection, etc.). this is also referred to in guideline 8 (investment in human resource development), even if it is not specific and particularly emphatic. there is a lot of debate about health in europe and the world, and no single position, but it is also important to see that europe's competitiveness can be ensured by maintaining a balanced subsistence level in the long term - with a healthier and more active population. please, the european institutions, think further about the strategy and its implementation in this light.
in writing. the eu2020 strategy should promote the 'green revolution' of the european economy, resource-efficient, environmentally friendly and sustainable economic restructuring and green innovation. but progress cannot be about supporting the already above-average regions of europe; we must concentrate a significant part of development on supporting the most backward, most disadvantaged regions. there is still a huge gap in economic efficiency between the eastern and western halves of europe. for example, bulgaria needs three times as much energy to produce a unit of gdp as germany, which further reduces the country's competitiveness if energy prices rise. in supporting the green economy transition, the european union must not forget about existing and proven community policies such as the common agricultural policy and cohesion policy. the cap will also be needed to meet the objectives of protecting the environment and combating climate change. the best farmers in rural europe are the farmers themselves. the eu2020 strategy cannot succeed without cohesion policy. cohesion policy provides all the tools and flexibility needed for an eu economic development policy. cohesion policy instruments can support the achievement of any policy objective, so we can promote the development of renewable energy sources, increase energy and resource efficiency, and encourage green innovation.
i simply do not understand, as a human rights lawyer, what added value is there in terms of protecting the human rights of european citizens by the european union joining the same human rights convention that every single eu member state has already joined. let me make some connections, because i can see that non-lawyer colleagues are being misled here. here's the deal. there is a really important human rights instrument, by the way, called the european convention on human rights. this is not a convention of the european union, but of our quasi-twin brother, the council of europe. if a state infringes the rights of its citizens enshrined in the convention, they may bring an action before the european court of human rights in strasbourg to seek redress or legal redress against their own state. this is a possibility, as everyone knows, for citizens of all eu member states today. i can hardly imagine that there is a member of this house who does not know of a case in which someone in his member state threatened to take me all the way to strasbourg, and he did, winning a case against his own member state. so what's new, what's the added value, besides someone from the eu network getting a well-paid judgeship in strasbourg.

article 11 of the european convention on human rights defines the right to trade union activity as a human right. in direct contradiction to this provision, last week the hungarian military criminal court severely punished szima judit, the head of a union of ten thousand police officers, for activities that, whether viewed from a layman's or a lawyer's professional point of view, fell exclusively within the sphere of protecting the interests of workers and trade union activity. i emphasize that this did not happen in some third world banana republic, but in a member state of the european union. we are constantly talking here in this chamber and in the committees about the fact that the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon has strengthened the european union's commitment to human rights and made the protection of human rights more effective. i ask that the case of szima judith be a test case, a case study, that we all follow through on how, with the application of european human rights standards, this brave woman can regain the justice, dignity and livelihood she lost.
i apologize if i sound a little more personal than usual, but while we were talking about the cancer program here, and many people were emphasizing how terrible the disease is, how many people die from it, i was forced to think back to when i was diagnosed with cancer four years ago, on this day of the year, and i think i broke some kind of record, as they said in the oncology department. i had 14 tubes hanging out of me from head to toe, i was in intensive care for weeks, and now here i am, a member of the european parliament, raising my three children, living a full life. with my own story, i would like to encourage my female colleagues in particular, but also all european citizens, to do screenings, and with my own story i would like to send a message to my fellow citizens who are now sick, and to their relatives and healers, that they should never give up hope, and i wish them much strength, and i think of them with love and compassion.
in writing. the crisis unfolding in some euro area countries is calling on european decision-makers to protect public money, the community's finances, unconditionally in a way that has never been seen before. the economy and international markets are extremely sensitive to any action taken by the union - such as the measures adopted in today's rescue packages. whole economies could be at risk if we do not continue to manage the crisis at european level in a way that is proportionate to its gravity, so it is no exaggeration to say that the eyes of the world are on our finance ministers and the european institutions. in some eu member states, oligarchic governments, driven by private interests, have driven their economies to the brink of bankruptcy and made promising futures inaccessible to entrepreneurs, families and educated young people. on this basis, the rapporteur's intention to increase the accountability of the member states can only be welcomed. today, this is one of the key questions of successful crisis management. the end of the era of corrupt governments is needed more than ever in this time of crisis.

the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted an opinion on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) on the establishment of a european monetary fund. - my fellow members of parliament! the committee's opinion on the report took the view that the european investment bank had a well-researched crisis management in 2008. what do you expect from a development bank in a time of crisis? firstly, it is trying to compensate for the decline in lending activity of commercial banks by creating liquidity. it can be said that since 2008 the eib has steadily increased the size of its soft loans and has committed to provide an additional €15 billion per year for smes in 2009 and 2010. why is that so important? of course, because they are the biggest job creators, and we in europe have the biggest problems with employment right now. we need to create jobs. the bank also did a good job of analysing, precisely in this respect, how it had re-grouped the different target areas. the bank has made a significant contribution to helping smes to find their place in the world as quickly as possible. the committee would like to recommend to president maystadt three areas where we believe the bank could be made more efficient and transparent. the first is to increase the bank's focus on the countries most affected by the crisis, thereby also increasing the union's internal cohesion. secondly, the bank should not only partner with large commercial banks to finance smes, but also involve regional banks and savings banks, as these institutions have the best knowledge of the markets. finally, as a third point, we consider it necessary that the financial institutions in the partner group are obliged to transfer at least 20% of the profits from the eib's 50% total financing to the smes financed, i.e. more than what is required by the current contracts. overall, therefore, the eib has been well tested and has been at the forefront of responding to the crisis, but further joint efforts are needed with the european commission and the european development bank to make progress, particularly in the area of job creation.

in writing. - i would like to congratulate my colleague astrid lulling on the adoption of her report, which represents an important step forward in solving a problem that has been going on for a very long time. the 1986 council directive was unable to resolve satisfactorily the situation of spouses and partners of selfemployed persons who contribute to the pursuit of the activity and to the profits of the undertaking without having the status of employees or partners. it was therefore appropriate to repeal that legislation and to introduce a new directive establishing the recognition of the work of assisting spouses and their social security protection at the same level as that of entrepreneurs. another important result of the rapporteur and the european parliament is that, contrary to the original council proposal, the scope is no longer limited to self-employed activity in agriculture for income-generating purposes, as this target group also constitutes the majority of those working in, for example, small and medium-sized enterprises or in trade. although the council did not accept several important proposals by the rapporteur and the parliament, such as leaving the member states to provide an equivalent level of social protection for spouses and partners and ensuring that member states can maintain restrictive rules relating to certain social security schemes or levels, the proposed directive as a whole is nevertheless a significant step forward.

in writing. the recovery from the economic crisis is very bumpy, and the euro area has been shaken by fiscal problems in greece and other member states. all of this has raised the question of whether europe can compete with the economic power of the united states or china. the eu's 2020 strategy must therefore be an effective response to the economic and financial crisis, and a means of keeping up the economic strength of this bloc that is the lifeblood of 500 million people, so that the 27 do not fall behind their economic and political rivals. the complexity of today's challenges calls for an integrated policy toolbox. the european union should attach high importance to research and innovation in order to address challenges such as climate change and increasing global competition. and as it has become more important than ever in the post-crisis period to stimulate growth and job creation through research and innovation, i propose that this be set as the number one objective of the eu 2020 strategy. however, we have reason to fear that the current budget does not sufficiently cover the financial needs to meet the challenges of the 21st century. as a member of the committee on budgets, i am calling on the european commission to come forward with a bold, ambitious and feasible new budgetary model to make the eu 2020 strategy a success.
in writing. perhaps the most important task ahead is to set the 2020 strategy on the road to aligning member states' economic and employment policies on common principles. i would like to welcome the fact that, in addition to the sustainable social market economy, environmental protection and innovation aspects, the strategy places a strong emphasis on combating poverty and exclusion and strengthening social cohesion, which is a prerequisite for the former and one of the main pillars of the programme of the spanish-belgian-hungarian presidency trio. i consider it an important step forward that the european commission, in the second half of its integrated guidelines for the strategy, sets ambitious and quantified targets in this area, both for employment of the working age population at 75% and for a 25% reduction in the number of people living below the national poverty line. in order to monitor and control the achievement of these objectives and to assess the fight against social exclusion, it is worth considering the application of the 'laeken indicators' adopted by the laeken european council in 2001 and their components, which have been extended and further detailed in the meantime. the laeken indicators provide a detailed and reliable picture of the situation of certain groups in society in certain segments of the public sector and have been successfully used in recent years by a number of eu institutions, including eurostat.

in writing. in today's debate, the european parliament must take a stand on the very important question of how to transform the current statistical reporting system under eurostat in order to avoid similar cases to the greek scandal in the future. i would like to remind you that the greek fraud was exposed in 2004 and that the socialist hungarian government was also cheating during the same period, but the union has not taken any effective steps to reform the statistical reporting system since then. i think it is a good thing that the commission is considering giving eurostat audit powers, but i am convinced that we must go further than that because the future of the single currency is at stake. what do i see as the opportunity to move on? the excessive deficit procedure should not only penalise member states that have repeatedly breached their commitment to the 3% deficit target set by the maastricht treaty, but also those that have been misleading investors and the union for years by providing false statistics, thus endangering the stability of the euro area. i agree with the trend that staff of national statistical offices should take personal responsibility for the quality of the data provided to eurostat. i therefore propose that, in the light of the work of the council working party chaired by herman van rompuy, we ask the commission to draw up a much stricter system of sanctions than the current one.

in writing. the further enlargement of the schengen area is positive and should be supported by all means, as it will now make one of the most important of the union's achievements, the free movement of citizens, available to all eu citizens. this is a tangible benefit for citizens today in their everyday lives. the schengen acquis is particularly important for us, the citizens of the new member states, who have suffered from decades of isolation. i am therefore pleased that in the near future another section of the schengen external borders in hungary may be dismantled in relation to another neighbouring member state, romania, and that citizens living on both sides of the border, including the one and a half million hungarians in transylvania, can freely participate in a europe without borders. under the preparation timetable for romania and bulgaria, these two eu member states could join the schengen area in the first half of 2011. so this is during the hungarian presidency. it is important that this schedule is implemented as planned, so that the mid-term evaluation milestones are also successful. the current - intermediate - assessment should remove all legal obstacles so that technical preparations for the integration of the two countries can begin in this area in the second half of the year. i support the rapporteur's assessment and conclusions, particularly that the european parliament and in particular the members of the libe committee should have access to the evaluation reports and relevant information before they deliver an informed opinion.
in writing. the accession of bulgaria and romania to the schengen system next year could be a major success of the hungarian eu presidency, which takes office in january. the decision will increase security in the region and will ensure that millions more, including hungarians, will be able to travel freely across europe without border controls. i welcome the positive assessment of the readiness of the two member states in the final report of the schengen assessment working party, which was also taken into account by the inter-state council. as a result, bulgaria and romania are expected to be technically ready to join the schengen information system as of october 2010. following the final evaluation, scheduled for december, the two countries are expected to join the europe without frontiers from next spring. the delay resulting from the development of the schengen information system shall not adversely affect the accession of the countries awaiting integration. it is of particular importance for hungary that its neighbours, including members of the hungarian community across the border, enjoy the freedom to travel across the union without any border controls.
two years after the crisis, economic growth, albeit slow, has started. however, the situation remains extremely fragile. the enormous public deficits and the debt burden of the member states require a highly disciplined budgetary policy and austerity everywhere. there is no room for populist promises. we must maintain and even reduce the deficit everywhere. the challenge is to ensure that austerity does not leave the poor, the needy, in a hopeless situation. it is important to guarantee that no one in the future will follow the greek example. it is very good that mr barroso and mr olli rehn have also warned those who, by falsifying the real situation, have imagined a greek state bankruptcy and are playing with fire. it is therefore essential that member states' budgetary plans are subject to prior control by the commission. it's a pity that britain is not a partner in this. meaningful steps are also needed to establish genuine economic union. on the one hand, this means much more effective coordination of the economic policies of the member states. only community solutions can lead out of the union's crisis, national egoism, inward looking will only deepen the problems.
the g20 summit is expected to address global climate protection and, in this context, the carbon tax, alongside international financial regulation. the european commission itself is increasingly involved in the introduction of this tax in the eu, and the bp rig accident is giving further impetus to the debate on this issue. it is therefore worthwhile to lay down some principles. first, when it comes to the carbon tax, we should not forget that there are already market-based climate protection instruments, just think of the eu emissions trading system. any introduction of emission taxes is only possible in sectors that are not already part of the ets, which is increasingly covering a number of sectors. the aim is to avoid multiple taxation. second, a carbon tax cannot be a mere revenue-generating tool to help governments fill deficit budgets. its introduction should be supported only if it brings substantial environmental benefits and does not impose an undue burden on the population and on european industry. thirdly, the primary condition for the introduction of a carbon tax at community level is that other advanced industrial states follow suit. therefore, as with global financial regulation, the basic legal framework in this area needs to be established. the g20 summit may be a good opportunity to do so.
in writing. the eu-russia summit two weeks ago was less focused on energy security than previous summits. that's not to say that there haven't been some remarkable developments in the last few months. the merger of russian gas company gazprom and ukrainian gas company naftogaz has recently resurfaced. the president of ukraine offered to include representatives of the european union in future negotiations on the unification. however, as we have heard from the news, the european commission sees no reason to participate in the negotiations. we, unlike the european commission, believe that eu representatives should be present at the merger negotiations if invited. first of all, because gas imports, which account for one fifth of the total energy consumption, are delivered to the member states via naftogaz's networks. the merger of the two companies would thus have a fundamental impact on the functioning of the european energy market and thus on price competition. the merger of gazprom and naftogaz cannot be seen as the exclusive internal affair of two companies, since we know that the governments involved are also heavily involved in the negotiations prior to similar transactions. if that's what it's called, the eu should be at the table. if the union is not able to have a say in decisions affecting its own import volume, then the common energy policy is nothing more than useless, empty words.
in writing. – russia still sees the west as primarily responsible for the collapse of the soviet union and the chaos of the yeltsin years. however, russia faces a number of intractable problems, which ought to impel it towards the west, but moscow’s dislike of the eu persists. first, russia’s leaders have converted it into an energy-producing state, but, as energy resources are depleting, its economy is in serious trouble. the world financial crisis has led to a collapse of energy prices, resulting in a loss of revenue for russia. russia’s population is collapsing demographically; it can barely mobilise enough soldiers to defend the country. in the mostly muslim northern caucasus, russia is experiencing an upsurge of violence. this violence is being met by repression, which means the conflict will continue. china has begun to challenge russia in central asia, which moscow has long regarded as its own sphere of interest. finally, one area where russia continues to perform well is in intelligence operations, as witness the rapid restoration of russian power in ukraine this year. this is a signal for the west. russia will do what it can to enlarge its western security zone, regardless of the interests of the eu.
in writing. - members of the house! i would like to welcome the recent european parliament resolution on the europe 2020 strategy, which calls on the european council to develop a forward-looking and coherent strategy to achieve the objectives of the initiative and to seek the ep's opinion when defining the key parameters. this consultation should also involve national parliaments, local authorities and relevant non-governmental bodies. in order to meet the labour market and poverty reduction objectives set out in the second half of the european commission's related integrated guidelines, a strong focus should be placed on the social inclusion of the continent's most populous and poorest minority, the roma, given the continuously increasing proportion of roma in the active layer of the social security system and the considerable economic potential of the integration of the large unemployed population into the labour market. the strategy should set out a detailed and sustainable roadmap for the integrated guidelines, in particular for raising employment rates for the working age population to 75%, reducing the number of people below the national poverty line by 25% and reducing the age-group drop-out rate from schooling to below 10% and, in line with parliament's resolution, setting a target of 100% for secondary education. however, it is regrettable that the main objectives of the strategy do not include equality between women and men, which is one of the pillars of the programme of the spanish-belgian-hungarian presidency trio.
mr president, this latest crisis has to be seen as a symptom of the profoundly difficult relationship between israel and palestine. why has it been impossible to find a solution to the conflict between them? the easy answer is to blame one side or other as being more responsible. both sides profess that they do indeed want a peaceful outcome, but what actually takes place says something else. it is far from evident that a solution acceptable to all is still a part of the mindset of either israel or the palestinians. indeed they both perceive their interests as being best served by the conflict because they believe any solution would result in their ending up in a weaker position that what they have currently. from the israeli perspective, although formally there is support for a two-state solution, it is hard to see an israel that could live on equal terms with a palestinian state. such a state would pose an insurmountable security problem. from the palestinian perspective – that of hamas above all – peace would mean the end of its monopoly power in gaza, and having to share the state with fatah. hence we can see that the present troubled situation is the least bad option for all the involved parties. until they are given incentives to find an option that is superior to the present, the conflict will continue, tragically.
vice-president of the commission/high representative. − mr president, i am extremely pleased to have the opportunity to discuss human rights in this house. on many occasions, the house has stated its commitment to this subject. it is a commitment that i fully share. for the european union, human rights matter. they are at the core of our identity and they are at the heart of what we do around the world. our own history of entrenching human rights, democracy and the rule of law across 27 member states is a success story and acts as a source of inspiration to others. so it is logical that we have developed a strong set of mechanisms for promoting these values in different contexts – as the report on human rights and democracy in the world sets out. to give just one example, over the last 18 months we have provided eur 235 million in funding for 900 ngo projects in 100 countries. this work is extremely important and it must continue. but we are not the only actor and ours is not the only economic and political success story. therefore, as the world changes, we also need to make sure that we ask ourselves what we could do better. while human rights are universal, a ‘one size fits all’ approach does not work. that does not mean we should change the message on the importance of human rights, but there is room to be smarter in how we deliver it. we will make most progress if we approach the human rights agenda in a focused and realistic way. we need to target our efforts, tackling each case based on a detailed understanding of the country at hand. we also need a sharper focus on results. previous resolutions of this house have called for more information to better assess the effectiveness of our policies. i share your concern. we must judge our efforts by outcomes, even if our contributions to improving human rights situations are an investment over the long term. the work of the human rights subcommittee and its chair, mrs hautala, on human rights defenders is an extremely good example of this and i very much welcome the report and its findings and pay tribute to those who have worked so hard on it. let me address some specific aspects by making clear that i will continue to meet civil society actors and human rights defenders, both in brussels and abroad, as i have done in gaza, in china and, most recently, in brussels. i expect colleagues in delegations and here in brussels to do the same. a good example of being smart and well-organised has been our work on promoting the ratification of the rome statute in the run up to the kampala conference this year. we have worked with specific countries, offering eu support, at delegation and headquarters levels, working with member states and the eu presidency. to name just one success story, when i visited the seychelles last month, i discussed a range of issues on which they and the eu could strengthen our cooperation, with piracy as a top priority. i also raised the icc and encouraged the president, mr michel, to submit the ratification bill to parliament. upon my return to brussels, i was pleased to receive a letter confirming that the government has now launched this process. looking ahead, i want to see what more we can do to support the abolition of the death penalty worldwide. i want to assure this house that work on abolishing the death penalty is a personal priority for me. i will see to it that work advances, both bilaterally and in multilateral fora, beginning with the united nations in september. as this house knows, the promise of the lisbon treaty is a more coherent, more consistent and hence more effective eu foreign policy. this is also a chance for our work on human rights, democracy and the rule of law. these will run like a silver thread through everything that we do externally. the external action service, once in place, will give us the chance to realise our potential and strengthen our ability to speak with one voice. with its integrated structure, the new service should help to ensure that human rights issues are reflected in all areas of our external action, including csdp, development and trade. in this respect, let me refer to the oral question on trade in goods used for torture. the eu takes its commitment to the fight against torture very seriously. any shortcomings in the implementation of regulation (ec) no 1236/2005 must – and will – be addressed. in that spirit, we have invited amnesty international and the omega research foundation to present their findings to a meeting later this month of the regulatory committee with our member states. on human rights, as in other areas, we have to pull together. we need the continued commitment of member states, of this house and of the other eu institutions. we need to review the work we do regularly and to make sure that we get the best use of all available instruments – from human rights dialogues to eu guidelines, from the european instrument for democracy and human rights to our bilateral assistance and our actions in multilateral fora. revisiting our overall eu human rights strategy could be a useful exercise, and i intend to launch a consultation process to inform the development of a new human rights strategy this year. honourable members, dear friends, eleanor roosevelt used to say, ‘it is better to light a candle than curse the darkness’. this is valid for our human rights policy too. in the european union we have many tools to help make the world a better place. we need to mobilise and connect them better. that is what i want to do, maximising the potential of the new lisbon set-up and continuing to count on your vital support.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, as shadow rapporteur on behalf of the ppe group, and also as my group’s coordinator for the subcommittee on human rights, first of all i would like to thank ms hautala for her great work on this report. as the votes in the human rights subcommittee showed in april, the report enjoys great support across different groups, as the issue of protection of human rights remains of joint interest to us all. we reached good compromises, taking into account the different points of view. the eu has already worked out mechanisms and tools of great value so, as i have already emphasised in previous discussions, we have to make sure that we achieve better implementation of the existing guidelines, with the evaluation of existing policies developing more effective protection. finally, i strongly encourage member states to show stronger political will to support the action of human rights defenders, as the report highlights. with the lisbon treaty in place, it is important that the protection and security of human rights defenders be made a priority issue in the eu relationship with third countries and integrated at all levels of the union’s foreign policy, in order to increase the coherence, effectiveness and credibility of the eu’s support for human rights.
human rights defenders deserve special attention and, in addition to that, we need to help them in practice, so that they are not only respected and given attention when it is too late. i think it is important that parliament should give its opinion on this matter and i congratulate mrs hautala on her initiative and her excellent work. the report summarises in detail the steps that need to be taken, so i would just like to focus on what the union needs to do to be able to provide effective and very rapid assistance to human rights defenders in need. it is precisely efficiency and speed that in many cases are what they need most to protect their personal health or even their lives. supporting the work of human rights defenders is only one aspect of the protection of human rights, so it is important that human rights play an important role in the content of the structure of the european external action service and that the human rights mainstream in particular transfers this work, and this is what the lisbon treaty, as mrs ashton said, has made possible by the lisbon treaty. now it's your turn, it's up to you to create the practical conditions.
member of the commission. − madam president, let me start where this very useful debate has ended. i think we are united in our messages. the draft resolution has generated a debate which was about these united messages. i think it shows our joint commitment but it also underlines the seriousness of the situation we face in bosnia and herzegovina. i think it is clear from this debate – and i fully concur with this – that politicians, above all politicians in bosnia and herzegovina, have to resume the responsibilities that they have vis-à-vis the citizens and vis-à-vis the country. i also fully agree that the best way to prove it is through concrete progress. again, the case was made that visa liberalisation has shown that there is a way for the politicians in bosnia and herzegovina not only to agree on something, but actually to do a very good job in implementing a rather demanding road map leading us to the visa-free regime. i think we are also clear on what the short-term and the medium-term priorities are. regarding the short-term priorities, firstly there is definitely the compliance of the bosnia and herzegovina constitution with the european convention on human rights and the saa agreement; secondly, the adoption of the state-level census law; and, thirdly, the fulfilment of the outstanding benchmark for visa liberalisation. at the same time, i do not think we should just wait for the elections to bring certain results in october. we need to start thinking now about the medium term – a holistic, but at the same time comprehensive approach to bosnia and herzegovina. already now we should work on momentum on the window of opportunity, which should be presented by the results of the october elections. i think we need to encourage the politicians during the pre-election campaign to clearly state their vision of the future of bosnia herzegovina, their view of the european future and the european relationship and aspirations of that country. there will be many challenges after the elections, including further changes in constitutions, and we need to make sure that both of us – the politicians in bosnia and herzegovina and the eu – are ready for the greater part of the ownership to be assumed by the politicians in bosnia and herzegovina. i think it is time for these elections to end the dayton era and put us in a european era.

member of the commission. − mr president, sincere thanks to all the speakers for the comments they have made. despite the late hour there is very clearly considerable interest in many of the topics. it has been quite rightly mentioned that europe and the united states together represent close to 60% of global aviation traffic. therefore this agreement is very significant: not only for the citizens of europe and the united states but for all the passengers that take advantage of our carriers. in addition to the face-value significance for air transport, the unparalleled business links, strong demographic and cultural ties and geographic positioning mean that the us, for europe, is the most important strategic aviation partner. it is exactly the objective of this agreement to consolidate this strategic transatlantic partnership and to secure the gains from the first stage agreement guaranteeing a stable framework for future relations. a number of speakers pointed out the need to continue this process of engagement so we can make further advances. one of the achievements of the second stage agreement is indeed that it puts in place a process of engagement. i would like to immediately reply at this point that of course the commission would be keenly interested in sharing our own work in the joint committee with parliament, as we see a significant value in parliament’s engagement as we move forward. by opening up more opportunities for the industry and addressing some of the regulatory challenges it faces, the agreement will play a role in helping the european aviation industry emerge from the current period, which is characterised by profound economic and operational challenges. let me be very clear. the commission, like many of the speakers, agrees that the second stage agreement is not perfect. it is a very important step forward but it is not a perfect or conclusive step. like the speakers here, the commission would have liked to have seen an immediate and irrevocable change to the us law governing ownership and control of their airlines, but the reality is that this is not in the hands of the us administration, with whom we negotiate. it is a legislative reform that can only be delivered with the support of the us congress. this leads me to a very important message to this audience: that europe will have to use its influence to persuade the us congress of the merits of continuous reforms. in this respect in particular, the european parliament will have a key role to play, with the engagement and the opportunities parliament has with congress in relations with members of congress. i hope that we can count on you to advance the dialogue, the discussions and, most importantly, the legislative steps needed. on the issue that was pointed out by a couple of speakers on data protection: just for the record, this is not part of the air services agreement. it is a matter of justice and home affairs the following point is the commission statement on the implementation of the first railway package directives [2010/2556 ((rsp) ].
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank the parliament again for this very useful engagement on an issue which is actually quite emotional from time to time, because we all want to see an integrated european railway system in our lifetime. hopefully we will all live long enough to do so. in the past i was engaged in the restructuring of railways in two countries that are now eu member states. i know how very difficult it is to move forward a system that has long traditions and an enormous amount of vested interests surrounding it. yet i think we all need to persevere because an integrated and highly efficient railway system is absolutely essential for the well-being of europeans and also for the competitiveness of our region. we, in the commission, take your comments very seriously and we will continue to work to move forward on the initiatives that require success in this area. i do not think it is a paper tiger if we judge by the number of infringement procedures. why has it taken so long? it has taken so long because there has been a process of engagement with member states. there was also an enlargement that brought new elements into the system and there has been an attempt by the commission to make this engagement meaningful – in other words to send serious questionnaires, to analyse the responses carefully and then to follow up with member states. so the result has legs, and bites! infringement procedures are not an objective in itself. what we are aiming at is a well-functioning railway market able to provide more numerous, safer, consumer-oriented services to our citizens and our undertakings. with this in mind, the recast of the first railway package will be designed to simplify and clarify the existing provisions with a view to facilitating their implementation, but also to modernise them. i would like to make two points in response to comments and questions put to us. one is on the level of safety. the data is categorical that the level of safety has actually increased since the market was opened but, since we are talking about people’s lives, even if we have one accident it is one too many, so obviously we need to strive to increase this level of safety. since in two comments a question was posed regarding the imposition of public service obligations, i would like to clarify that the first package is not an obstacle to that. these obligations are regulated under separate legislation and therefore pursuing them is a possibility. many members spoke about strategy and directions. i will share with the parliament our three main priorities – and they reflect many of the comments that have already been made. firstly, improving market access conditions and making them more transparent and non-discriminatory, secondly establishing a regulatory framework, fostering both public and private investments in the rail sector and, thirdly – something that many of you spoke about – reinforcing the regulatory supervision of the rail market to ensure fair competition between the operators and, ultimately, lower costs for those who use the service. the commission will engage the ministers in dialogue on these matters, in the permanent form of ministers of transport. the rail sector has been a focus of attention and we will make sure that it remains so and actually expands its presence on their agenda. we will soon adopt the recast proposal so it may be tabled in parliament by september and we look forward to a very fruitful cooperation with you on the forthcoming legislative process, and certainly to visible and tangible results within our lifetime.

mr. president, and madam commissioner. allow me, my fellow members, to be the first to dispense with the subject. if someone had told me 20 years ago that bishop lászló tőzső would be speaking to tomás deutsch, mep, as vice-president of the european parliament, i would have listened with disbelief, but fate and the ways of the creator are unfathomable. i am grateful to be able to work with mr president here in the european parliament. i welcome you, and the vice-president of the european parliament, a man who, alongside jerzy buzek, embodies two very important european values: action against dictatorships that undermine the dignity of the individual and loyalty to common european christian values. my fellow members of the house of representatives! let me personally express my sympathy to the victims and survivors of the floods in central europe and, of course, to the victims of the disasters in france. but it's also about the tone of the thank you. thank you to all those who have personally taken part in the flood protection and thank you to our fellow europeans who have already mobilized their personal financial resources and volunteered to help with the protection and help with the recovery. due to a special situation, thanks is of course an inaccurate expression, a special situation is the cause of the floods in hungary, since in hungary rivers and smaller streams have emerged from their sources to a significant extent, which previously did not cause such disasters. this also underlines the fact that traditional flood control measures are no longer sufficient. i would like to express my appreciation for the european commission's swift and effective action in defence of the european institutions and i would like to express my hope that, like the european parliament, the european commission will decide swiftly and effectively on the necessary financial assistance to assist in the recovery.

member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank all who have spoken this evening. i will be travelling to poland next week, and many of your comments give me a much deeper understanding of what i will be looking for. let me take first the issue that has been put brought up most frequently by all speakers. this is the ability of the commission not only to mobilise emergency assistance and deploy teams when we are fighting a disaster, but also to alleviate the pain and suffering of people, post-disaster, by providing access to the solidarity fund. i would like to make two points on this. first, what do we have today? many of the speakers have clearly been engaged and interested in how the solidarity fund operates, so let me just recap what people have said so far. there is a threshold of 0.6% of gdp or at least eur 3 billion in damages. just by way of illustration, for poland this is a damages threshold of eur 2.1 billion; for hungary it is eur 591 million; for the slovak republic, eur 378 million. only after that threshold has been passed is there access to the fund. the submission has to be made 10 weeks after the disaster and then, on the basis of the submission, the solidarity fund is triggered. as many of you have probably noticed in the past, sometimes the response period – the time it takes for money to reach the beneficiaries – while intended to be shorter, can sometimes stretch into the future. so that is the reality today. my second point is that the commission has made a proposal to the council which was also strongly supported – even before these very serious floods – by parliament. the proposal aims precisely to increase flexibility, accelerate speed, and allow local circumstances to be better factored into the response. however, this proposal has been stuck in the council, mostly because of financial considerations in the context of the crisis. in the commission we are very hopeful that the floods will flush it through, and our intention is to take into account the experience of the floods and improve the proposal further. i would certainly be very grateful to parliament for your support. we will echo the words we have heard this evening on what you would like to see by way of solidarity. so a friend in need can be a friend indeed. we believe that is really essential and we are very much in favour of pursuing it. we will, given the circumstances and given the messages we have heard this evening, do everything possible within the current parameters of the solidarity fund to pursue the delivery of assistance as expeditiously as we can. the fund is not my area of responsibility but i will work with commissioner hahn who, like me, is very keen to see the fund being flexible and agile so that we can deliver now but also deliver better in the future. secondly, comments were made – in addition to the solidarity fund – on being able to tap into the structural funds and the cohesion fund. yes, indeed the eu financial instruments can cofinance a wide range of risk management measures. they can cofinance many of the things people talked about – such as reconstruction of river beds, afforestation, allowing a natural flow of water to return, planning, and multi-country studies. just as an illustration of what kind of funding is currently available: for poland eur 700 million are available and for the slovak republic, eur 120 million. obviously it is very important to look into current allocation and opportunities to use these funds in a flexible manner to provide relief to those who are affected. thirdly, there has been quite an important discussion on the floods directive. it is regrettable to say that some countries that are most severely hit by the floods have not transposed it yet. there are infringement procedures open, including against poland, and i do hope that what has happened is a wake-up call so that we will see member states moving very prudently on the implementation of the floods directive. dates have been mentioned of 2011, 2013 and 2015, where very critical deadlines have to be met. i have heard a number of speakers raise the issue of prevention, which is really much better than cure. in our communication which will appear at the end of this year we will be taking this comprehensive approach of combining prevention measures with response and feedback mechanisms through rehabilitation. without this kind of approach i think we will be sitting in this room in the future discussing the same issues the same way we are doing tonight. that takes me to the much broader issue, namely adaptation to climate variability and climate change. climate change is here to stay. honourable members have talked about mosquitoes returning. this sounds like an impossibility. were they not supposed to be gone? well, with climate change we may see the return of malaria even to the european continent. of course the only way to cope is to very prudently assess what the trends are and what we can do to be best prepared for those trends. climate systems are not linear. in other words we have to be prepared for a wide variety of scenarios and this is what in my team we would like to bring forward so that our action can be as effective as possible. let me raise one more point, on insurance schemes. regions of the world that have been at risk of climate variability for longer than us in europe – especially latin america and the caribbean – have invented insurance schemes for risk mitigation that i believe are of interest to our region. as we think of advances in the financial industry in europe, i think it is important to encourage innovation that would allow us to package risk better, reduce it and provide more assurances to those who suffer from disasters. today we are talking about floods. i would not be at all surprised if later in the summer we were talking about forest fires. all those risks require us to think in a way that collectively reduces the burden of risk on those that are most exposed. in the next months i will certainly come to parliament as we evolve our thinking on how we can best prepare and equip europe to respond to disasters. i will be sure to come to parliament often because what i have heard this evening – which incidentally relates to my country, romania and bulgaria are not entirely off the hook, with the danube river being higher than it normally is at this time of the year – is a tremendous level of concern that has to translate into reciprocity of an equally high level of action. may i just conclude, since many of the speakers have turned to you, mr president, by congratulating you on your election, and say that i am very honoured to be the first commissioner to stand in front of you in your new capacity.

in writing. - mr president, i have to go. last year, during the debate on the copenhagen climate conference, it was often predicted that one of the inevitable consequences of climate change would be more extreme weather events. we didn't have to wait long for all the things we've been talking about to become a near reality. countries in central europe have been hit by unprecedented flooding in recent weeks. hungary has not experienced such extensive, heavy rainfall in its history, with rainfall equivalent to twice or even three times the monthly average in four days. the problem is compounded by the fact that the huge masses of water have not been coming from the major rivers but from small and medium-sized streams that were previously considered gentle, and that have not been properly dammed in recent years. the enormous damage caused by the floods and the desperate struggle of local citizens warn decision-makers that all the water conservation investments needed to prevent similar disasters must be implemented urgently. "it's in the bathroom that you meet your friend", says an old saying. a union built on solidarity between the peoples of europe is fulfilling the dream of its founding fathers by helping those in need in such difficult situations. i therefore propose that the commission examine the possibility of using appropriate financial means to speed up the construction of flood reservoirs and other water conservation facilities in these countries.
in writing. the recent floods in central and eastern europe have also shown that in many cases people suffered not from a lack of people but mainly from a lack of a central and unified logistics system and service. it should be recognised that the protocols established so far in the field of civil protection and disaster management are neither sufficient nor adequate. a security system guaranteeing the safety of citizens' lives and property must be able to handle both modern information communication tools and services. access to information for people with disabilities in the event of a disaster is still limited, as was the case with the icelandic volcanic eruption and the subsequent air traffic chaos. this requires a change of attitude. it is essential that all media provide accessible, unobstructed (with captions, clearly visible visual information) information and assistance to people in a disaster situation, and mobile operators should also cooperate to provide information to subscribers on request and tailored to their needs, as free and as urgent as possible. to achieve this, the european commission needs to work with national governments on a determined initiative to further develop the standard protocols.
