madam president, we should not be complacent about our work in this area and should not look down on the united states. actually, on the one hand, the us is much closer to making comprehensive new legislation on financial sector regulation and supervision. they are also concentrating more on substance than on a turf war. the new legislation, which is before the us congress, envisages a consumer bureau with substantive powers to give rulings, including a total ban on specific financial products. standardised derivatives will be traded on established exchanges and forced through clearing-houses. hedge funds and private equity firms will have to register with the scc over and above a certain, very large capital limit. we are discussing how to distribute the powers between european institutions and national institutions. well, the solution is quite easy. the macroprudential institutions, like the ecsrb, should be closely linked with the european central bank, a european institution, but microprudential institutions need to be coordinated among national institutions.
the crisis has taught us all a hard lesson. we cannot allow financial institutions to engage in uncontrolled, speculative activities, endangering the safety of millions of jobs. the new supervisory institutions serve this purpose. i am very pleased that the compromise proposals to the council have included amendments which are important for the countries of central and eastern europe. for example, we have agreed to allow the governors of non-euro area central banks to participate in the governing bodies of the esrb and to apply the simple majority decision-making procedure to settle disputes between supervisors. the new supervisory institutions should be in place by 1 january 2011 at the beginning of the hungarian presidency. i believe that in order to achieve a compromise, both the council and the european parliament should make concessions wherever it is technically appropriate and justified. the council could thus allow the new authorities to have a single seat in frankfurt, to be subject to the european supervisory authorities for cross-border financial institutions or to operate independently of the commission. the ep could make concessions. you guys! i think it would be enough if instead of creating technical standards we were to deal only with strategic directions and finally the right to declare an economic emergency, that would be the responsibility of ecofin. i hope that along these lines a compromise can be found, and that we can reach a compromise by september at the latest, and that the institutions will indeed be in place by 1 january.

in writing. belgium took over the presidency of the eu council from spain at a difficult time. the european union is facing a series of major decisions, and the country holding the rotating presidency is preparing for a change of government. however, the executive cabinet can safely build on a reliable and proven cadre of civil servants and diplomats who have been consistently preparing for the presidency for years, and who draw on the experience accumulated during the country's dozens of consecutive presidencies. i consider it exemplary that, despite the many debates that divide the belgian language communities and political forces, the programme of the eu presidency is surrounded by national consensus. as a member of the presidency trio, belgium will act in the spirit of the joint spanish-belgian-hungarian programme and i trust that it will successfully fulfil its commitments. i welcome the fact that the president-in-office is embarking on a strong social programme for the next six months and that he intends to keep the social impact of the crisis under constant review and will keep it on the council's agenda. i support your plans for a conference on the rights of persons with disabilities and your efforts to promote european adoption of the un convention on the rights of persons with disabilities. he is waiting for belgium to draw the balance of the european year against poverty and exclusion. i hope that in the six months ahead the enlargement process will gain momentum, croatia will be closer to the conclusion of accession negotiations and the countries of the western balkans will also receive positive messages.
in writing. – we still are in an era of financial turmoil, requiring new and coordinated solutions; furthermore, continuing the advancement of the implementation of the lisbon treaty is still a task ahead. i am looking forward to working with the belgian presidency among such difficult circumstances. for one, on institutional issues, such as the creation of the european external action service, or the european citizens’ initiative, for which i am co-rapporteur in the european parliament. i hope that we will be able to elaborate a regulation that makes this tool of direct democracy accessible, easy to use and safe against abuse , giving an opportunity for european citizens who want to participate in european decision making. moreover, i am delighted to see that the belgian presidency has ideas concerning gender equality as well, as the presidency programme mentions most of the themes that the spanish presidency tried to advance. however, i would have been even more delighted if the belgian presidency had been more ambitious, or if it had only named expressis verbis the european protection order in its programme. i consider this piece of legislation highly important, naturally besides the new gender equality strategy and the women’s rights charter.
in writing. the second half of the 18-month spanish-belgian-hungarian trio is of particular importance for hungary. as a member of the trio and as the next presidency, we are ready to continue our close cooperation to date in order to promote the creation of a union that is closer to its citizens and their concerns, and able to respond swiftly and decisively to challenges. let me highlight just a few of these familiar semester tasks. i think it is important that the review of the eu budget is completed this year so that the debate on the next multiannual financial framework can begin under the hungarian presidency. secondly, the discussion on the financial supervision reform package must be closed. it would be a serious loss of face for all of us if the supervisory institutions were not in place on 1 january. thirdly, it is in the union's interest to conclude accession negotiations with croatia this year. i am convinced that if we have the political will, we can achieve this goal. this is also necessary to preserve the credibility of the enlargement process. finally, the preparations for romania and bulgaria to join the schengen area are at an end. free movement within the union is one of the most tangible achievements of the common europe for its citizens, so let us help it to become a reality in every way possible. i am confident that under the belgian presidency we will make substantial progress in all these areas.
in writing. an extraordinary council meeting will be held during the hungarian presidency in 2011, with energy policy issues primarily on the agenda. the belgian presidency should take decisive steps to strengthen the common energy policy before then. the step-by-step is perhaps the biggest investment. the strategic review presented earlier by the european commission has correctly identified the main development priorities. the negotiations on infrastructure should therefore continue to focus on the generation of financial resources. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. the new long-term budget should give a stronger role to basic energy infrastructure developments through the reform of the ten-e programmes. another important area of action is to position new technologies that affect energy supply. only through legal harmonisation at community level can decentralised energy production and renewables be given the necessary impetus. we have high hopes for the commission's proposal which would provide a new legal framework for smart energy networks facilitating bilateral communication. as this is a new regulatory area, political negotiations should start under the belgian presidency. in addition to looking ahead, the belgian presidency should monitor how the large body of legislation already adopted is implemented by the member states. the implementation of the third energy package, or the final version of the gas regulation, which will be adopted in the coming weeks, should not be delayed.
in writing. it is welcome that the belgian presidency is paying close attention to improving the situation of people with disabilities, with particular attention to monitoring the signing and ratification of the un convention on the rights of persons with disabilities in the member states. however, in my view, the council decision of 26 november last year already establishes the legal basis for the application of the un convention to the european institutions, including the european council, and thus for the belgian presidency to use the provisions of the convention as a reference point. given the significant differences in the initial and ratification processes in the various member states, i am firmly of the view that this should not cause the eu institutions to be uncertain about the de facto effects of the convention: there is no need for individual ratification by the member states at eu level, as the european council, with the representatives of the member states and a decision of the european parliament in support, has already taken a decision in this direction. despite the fact that my specific question on this subject two months ago - despite the very good working relationship with my spanish colleagues - has still not been answered, i have asked the committee responsible to put the question on the agenda. i hope that we will get an answer to this question under the belgian presidency. in conclusion, we wish our belgian colleagues good work and success.
madam president, being a human rights lawyer, i follow the accession process of iceland from the perspective of human rights – and, i must say, with great worry. i have just one minute to speak on the issue of human rights – mainly of course of those who challenge, question or protest against the accession – and would like to remind icelandic citizens of two important things. one is that you have a fundamental human right under modern human rights law to be informed correctly, precisely and accurately about public affairs – not just the positive sides but also the negative sides of accession. moreover, those of you who want to mobilise against accession have just the same right to freedom of speech, freedom of expression, and freedom of assembly as those who mobilise for the accession. you have just the same rights to public financial resources without discrimination, and access to public media, public television and so on. claim your rights before it is too late.
in writing. one of the most urgent tasks of the transposition of the lisbon treaty is the creation of a new, unified european union external action service (eeas). a strong and well-functioning foreign service requires careful and far-sighted preparation, cost-effective establishment and predictable and transparent operation. these conditions can be met if the creation of the new organisation is based on close teamwork between the european commission, the council and parliament and its competent committees, and on coordination of all the relevant details. one of the basic prerequisites for the establishment and operation of the eeas is to define the budget required by the new body and where and under what conditions it will receive the necessary funds. the new organisation will be effective if it has a clear and simple organisational structure, clear operating rules and human resource management issues. the eeas staff will be drawn from three sources: the eu institutions, the member states and, where appropriate, the host country. full equality between staff from the eu institutions and staff from the member states is needed during their service. it is equally important that the selection principle and practice in all three groups be based on transparent and uniform processes and requirements. it is also important that the disciplinary procedure be consistent with the practice of the european institutions, and this is ensured by the fact that there is no separate body dealing with the cases that fall within this area.

my fellow members of the house of representatives! as a member of the hungarian european parliament, i feel it is my duty to speak out on behalf of the hungarians in vojvodina and to draw not only the attention of the parties in the hungarian parliament, but also your attention to the fact that the rights of the relative hungarian majority living in vojvodina are unfortunately being seriously violated every day. the physical atrocities committed against them have become almost daily, and they dare not even file a complaint, fearing a repeat of the case of the temerin boys. they are restricted in their use of the language, and there is no hungarian administration in public offices. currently, the serbian authorities are organizing against the united hungarian youth camp, which has been regularly held for several years, and are trying to ban it. the situation threatens the complete extinction of the hungarian national feeling, the hungarian people. in order to solve this serious problem, the need to establish broad territorial autonomy in the region of bansko is indisputable.
mr president, i think the report on kosovo is very good but what i want to talk about is that the broad thrust of european policy towards the western balkans has long been the establishment of democracy and the promotion of stability. there are cases where the two do not coincide, where stability is actually the enemy of democracy because the status quo, though oppressive, guarantees stability in the short-term. for a long time this was the case with yugoslavia in general, and kosovo in particular. there was a general assumption that, even while yugoslavia was far from being a democracy, it was best to overlook the flaws because the alternatives were worse. with kosovo the opposite proved to be true. the treatment of the albanian population by the serbian authorities became intolerable, and an uprising followed. the outcome was limited western intervention, then the reluctant acceptance of the proposition that the people of kosovo would never again accept rule by belgrade. if serbia were to reassert its power over kosovo the result would be more stability, and independent status followed. but that can only be a first step. serbia has still to accept the loss of territory, which is painful. kosovo has itself begun the process of constructing democracy, but this will take time to mature, which is hardly surprising given the traumatic experience of its population, but neither democracy nor stability is helped by the refusal to recognise the independence of kosovo. recognition has to be the way forward.
president van rompuy recently made a tour of the western balkans. he also recognised that the integration of the countries of the western balkans was key to the future of the region. when he met with president boris tadic, he missed the slowdown in the integration process. now, if this is so important and if the process has really slowed down, then the countries concerned, kosovo, albania, have to do what they can to speed up the process, and they have to do what they can to make peace with serbia, to negotiate, to have a dialogue, to help speed up the integration. and europe should not have to double-check. the visa must be introduced in a sequential manner in all other countries.
madam president, the catastrophe at the deepwater horizon platform will remain probably as one of the biggest catastrophes in the 21st century in the history books of the world. it is about safety, security and prevention, and safety, security and prevention have a human face; all the industrial applications we use in any sector are human-designed, human-built and human-managed. therefore we have to make sure that industries throughout the sector and beyond the oil sector get the skills and knowledge they need to manage any consequences of any minor or bigger catastrophe in the future. i think we have to learn a lesson to continue to face the long-term challenge of the post-oil age by diversifying our resources and technologies. for us it must be crucial to rethink and support our strategic energy technology plan, including the deep sea technologies that were mentioned by mrs damanaki. finally colleagues, i have to say that this catastrophe, although it is not a natural catastrophe, has to be about solidarity and support. first, in order to re-establish nature, but then there needs to be solidarity and support for the employees of the company because the employees of the companies are victims – working elsewhere in the company. we have to go and ask for solidarity for the sector, within the sector, because we have seen that none of the sector’s players could solve the problem and we have to seek for solidarity within europe because bp is a european company; working people are there, european employees, and they have to have our solidarity to keep their working places for the future.

in writing. europe needs a strong and common agricultural policy beyond 2013. to do this, the eu's agricultural budget must be maintained at least at its current level. europe has a special responsibility to guarantee the world's food security. europe can produce the safest food possible, while maintaining the highest environmental standards. the cap's main task is therefore to preserve the agricultural production potential of europe. the amount of direct payments shall not be reduced as a result. in the case of rural development aid under the second pillar, it should be ensured that it is primarily intended for agricultural purposes. farmers should be protected against the extreme fluctuations in farm purchase prices observed in recent years. market-regulatory instruments and supply-containment measures in certain sectors, such as milk and wine, are needed to reduce losses to farmers caused by price fluctuations. european food trade is much more concentrated than food processors and farmers. a large supermarket chain is often confronted by hundreds of farmers who have a much worse bargaining position. therefore, closer cooperation between farmers should be promoted. however, this requires an exemption from the strict european competition rules in the field of agriculture. imports from third countries should be subject to the same strict environmental, food safety, animal welfare, etc. requirements as those applied to european producers.
in writing. a bold, in-depth reform of the cap is needed to take better account of the specific situation of the new member states. the cap can only be justified for european taxpayers and defended in the treasury if we can demonstrate that agriculture produces social and environmental public goods for society as a whole, in addition to food security in europe. only a substantial reform can save the cap! the agricultural developments in the new member states should be supported by preferential allowances, otherwise two (or in the case of romania and bulgaria) three-speed european agriculture could emerge. this would further deepen the gap between the eu15 and the 12 new member states. new investments will be needed, particularly in the new member states, to achieve a european green agriculture and the production of environmental public goods. instead of punishing them, farmers should be given financial incentives to purchase environmentally friendly machinery for soil-friendly farming, to exchange the genetic stock of cultivated varieties, to invest in environmental protection and to protect the cleanliness of the water supply.

in writing. mr barroso's speech just now also touched on the main tasks for energy policy. let me make three comments on this. first of all. we agree that infrastructure investment is essential. however, we still do not see the extent to which the european union is allocating community resources to implement them. connecting networks and the southern gas corridor are ambitious goals, but they will fade into obscurity if the union does not make financial commitments alongside the efforts of the private sector. the importance of the negotiations on the next seven-year budget cannot therefore be overstated. second of all. we do not see any backlog of job creation in the eu's energy strategy papers. it is crucial that investments that create jobs in addition to meeting the other criteria are given priority. the nearly four billion euro economic recovery plan did not meet this expectation. while ccs technologies with modest job potential have received generous support, energy efficiency investments that create most jobs only appeared for the first time in the complementary legislation. last but not least. it is a well-known fact that charging electric cars will become as commonplace in the eu as refuelling is today. however, achieving this also places great challenges on legislators. regulatory and standardisation barriers to renewable technologies must be removed and research and development programmes must be expanded.
in writing. the good news is that the current economic situation in the cee region is now also being assessed more favourably. it is too early to believe that the crisis is now completely behind us. hungary's economic prospects have also improved. however, the assessment of the current economic situation of the country has deteriorated somewhat. inflation expectations for the next six months have increased. accordingly, hungary is the only country where a reduction in short-term interest rates is expected only at a later stage. this negative trend is exacerbated by the appreciation of the swiss franc, as a large proportion of mortgage loans are denominated in this currency. all the states in the region are currently implementing austerity programmes, which place a heavy burden on social conditions. the best chance of success is expected to be in the czech republic. in contrast, the forecasts for hungary are cautious: the assessment is that the hungarian austerity package is unlikely to succeed. the substantive examination of the proposal submitted by the nine member states to the eu president is an important issue for the countries of the region. the proposal proposed would ensure that the public debt statement adequately reflects the costs of the pension reforms already implemented. based on adverse demographic trends, the commission's green paper on pensions calls for pension reform, while central european countries already implementing reforms are disadvantaged by the current methodology. i ask the european parliament to follow developments in this matter closely.
in writing. − as one of the shadow rapporteurs of the report i would like to congratulate dr parvanova on the acceptance of her report, which aims to offer solutions in order to minimize the disadvantage of minority women induced by ethnicity and reinforced by gender-specific structures. although gender equality is far from being achieved in mainstream society, roma women, compared to their non-roma counterparts, have lower life expectancy rates, lower education levels, significantly lower rates of employment and higher rates of poverty. in order to achieve full inclusion, gender statistics, indicators and benchmarks, as well as statistics broken down inter alia by gender and ethnicity, are crucial tools, and necessary if progress is to be properly measured. the collection of disaggregated data is a prerequisite for protecting and promoting the rights of ethnic minorities and must comply with member states’ rules on the protection of personal data. there is a very strong link between the level of education, the extent of economic activity and the probabilities of avoiding poverty for the whole family. that is why, in the first place, targeted policy actions are required to improve the access of roma women to education and registered employment.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, i am glad that the eu institutions appear so sensitive to the plight of the roma today. for that very reason, it is a pity that policymakers on all sides of the political spectrum seem to use the roma issue as a weapon against their counterparts. we all agree that freedom of movement is one of the fundamental principles of community law, and accept that this right is not unconditional. as the commission has confirmed, all eu countries have the right to take security measures regarding foreigners residing on their territory. the core values of the european union, such as non-discrimination, tolerance and solidarity, must be fully respected, and the expulsion of any eu citizens must be implemented on a case-by-case basis on the grounds of proper judicial decisions, or with the free, complete and informed consent of all the individuals concerned. to quote the commission again, ‘nobody should face expulsion just for being a roma’. political opinions and legal judgements are separate issues. we might find these expulsions distasteful or far-fetched, and we must draw attention to the safeguards and principles to be acted upon, but judging the legitimacy of the measures taken by france is the sole responsibility of the commission. large-scale repatriations might be repulsive, but are even more repulsive given the empty human rights lip-service of recent decades when technically nothing was done to alleviate the terrible poverty of the roma, except for the chanting of a few cold words about anti-discrimination and tolerance when it became politically handy. we, the european roma, reject the political misuse and misinterpretation of our issues. the roma must set the discourse about themselves to reveal the problems and to articulate what actions and measures have to be taken. as proclaimed several times by this parliament, and more specifically by the european people’s party, the poverty and social exclusion of the roma is a european issue and requires a strategy of its own; a common european solution is needed for a common european problem. that strategy must tackle the economic features of the social exclusion of roma and non-roma alike, such as structural unemployment, low qualifications, dwelling in seriously disadvantaged micro-regions and barriers to self-employment – all the issues which our people are escaping from when they migrate. the expulsion of the roma from france raises fundamental rights issues, as well as questions of fundamental values. there's been a lot of talk about that today. perhaps even more important politically, the stigmatization and criminalization of a vulnerable, especially poor, ethnic group has been a violation of the right to non-discrimination, but it can also trigger dangerous trends. president sarkozy has already found followers. in hungary, the far-right party jobbik is already calling for the stripping of hungarian roma citizenship and the internment of roma in camps. the mep from jobbik talks about police camps and forced integration. the commission has acted slowly, uncertainly and weakly over the summer. i have a few questions for you. what does the commission intend to do to combat the spread of hate speech, the increase in ethnic exclusion which is increasingly poisoning europe? when will there be a comprehensive european roma strategy to prevent countries from pushing the issue onto each other, and what does the commission intend to do to monitor the spending of european funds to see if they are really helping the roma effectively?
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, a lot has been said and there have been a lot of polemics. i shall not answer in the same way, because we have to calm down and look at what we are here for – namely to solve the problems and not to create more problems. one concrete question which was asked was: who was at the córdoba summit with the representatives of the roma? the answer is lászló andor and myself, two spanish ministers, a french state secretary and a finnish minister. that was all, from the 27 governments. if you look at the papers that are available on who spends what for the roma population, then you will understand that, in the main, our governments are not utilising funds to invest in a better life for the roma population, though i will leave my colleague, lászló andor, to speak on this. i will take up on the intervention by mr swoboda, because its tone was the same as that used by many members of the european parliament. i am astonished, because we share the same values and the same principles, and when i look at the resolution by the socialist party, it copies word for word what i said in the name of the commission in august. i will quote what i said then: ‘i regret that ... the rhetoric that has been used in some member states in the past weeks has been openly discriminatory and partly inflammatory. the situation of the roma is a serious matter. it should be on the agenda not just in august, but throughout the year, and it should be treated carefully and responsibly by policy-makers. national decision-makers have an important role to play to ensure both public order and the social integration of all europeans who choose to live within their territory. because europe is not just a common market – it is at the same time a community of values and fundamental rights. the european commission will watch over this.’ so that was the official declaration by the commission. however, the commission refuses to look at the roma question in black and white, and to make a party political issue of this. like you i condemned very clearly the rhetoric which has been utilised, not only in france, but in many other member states too. like you i believe that freedom of movement is one of the basic freedoms of our european union. the whole commission supports this. however, there are not only rights. there are also obligations, and the commission has the obligation to balance those rights and obligations, which were not put on the table by us, but which this house decided on in 2004, expressing the interests of the voters. in order to have this balance between rights and obligations put into practice we have been in contact on a daily basis with the french authorities. we have made this clear, and that is why the ministers came to brussels to have a very frank and very clear discussion with the commission. i have told you what the ministers told the commission. at the same time, our legal services are continuing to analyse what the facts are on the ground, because we cannot just declare war on a member state. these are rules for analysing what a member state has done, and i told you very clearly that this analysis has not yet finished and that we do not yet have all the proof of whether there was discrimination or not or whether the procedural guarantees were applied, following a case-by-case assessment, with a justified decision in writing and a one month period in which to leave. all this is still under analysis. we know – and these are hard facts – that france has not implemented the 2004 directive on that ‘big’ countries too can receive accusations from me and that i normally win these before the court – i will act. but in order to win before the court, one has to have serious grounds, you cannot member of the commission. − mr president, as in the past, i will also always be available in the future for discussing roma issues. let me concentrate my part of the answer on a few key points. at the beginning of this year, soon after this commission entered office, my first official visit was to paris at the opening conference of the european year for combating poverty and social exclusion. i was really impressed with the atmosphere and with the very sincere intention of ngos and government officials to tackle poverty and work together for what we later formulated as europe 2020 objectives. it should go without saying that the events of the last two months were not what we envisaged or encouraged in february in paris. this is a disappointment for many of us and i understand that part of this house which is very critical of these developments. however, even if we understand the very complex nature of these issues, i think we should turn our attention to the much more scandalous situation that exists in the countries of origin of the roma people who now face very difficult situations since france and other countries have decided to expel them. we are speaking about ten million people, many of them living in impossible situations. it is not true, as i heard in one of the statements today, that these people were never integrated. it is not true that the roma are culturally – or for any other reason – unable to integrate with mainstream societies. i have to state here that, although again it is a complex issue, before 1989 most of the roma people had jobs. they were very often poor and held unskilled jobs, but they were integrated to a certain degree in the labour market and had a basic level of livelihood. we have to make it very clear that the economic transition was also a disruption. the roma clearly became the main victims of this transitional period. if we do not appreciate this we will fail to understand the origin of the problem today and fail to appreciate how great the efforts needed are. these have to be european efforts because the countries affected do not have sufficient resources, energy and commitment to tackle the problems alone. i would like to reject categorically the assumption – or accusation – that the commission has only noticed now that some of this money – the social fund and the structural funds – does not reach the targets and does not deliver. we discussed this very frankly in córdoba with the involvement of george soros. we discussed it in the parliamentary conference chaired by mr swoboda; and there are other meetings which are destined to tackle this. the two-day esf conference in june also gave attention to this matter, as has the conference currently taking place in budapest. similar conferences are scheduled for bulgaria and slovakia next year. the main one, in which president băsescu is also expected to participate, will take place in october in bucharest and it will exclusively focus on how the european funds could be better used to tackle these problems. we have been very active, together with parliament, in establishing the new micro-finance facility. one of the key arguments for the micro-finance facility was the fact that marginalised communities – and particularly the roma – are not reached sufficiently and assisted by the mainstream financial sector or even by the european funding facilities. but we need to have a long-term strategy. nobody should believe that there is a silver bullet – some kind of quick fix to this problem – and that it is just a matter of finding a quick solution. we have to have a long-term strategy. we have a long-term strategy, namely europe 2020 with a strong commitment to combating poverty and numerical targets. the member states are working on their own reform programmes. it will simply not be acceptable if, in the countries where roma live in high numbers, a strong commitment is not made in the poverty reduction programme for the roma communities in terms of employment and, equally importantly, education. early childhood education is key. commissioner vassiliou is with us in spirit in all these discussions, and will also participate in the taskforce suggested by viviane reding when it comes into force. it starts with early childhood education and continues with a proper preparation for participating in the labour market. but it really is an uphill struggle and it will take a lot of energy. however, i would agree with all those who emphasise that we should also take the immediate situation seriously and not only speak about the long-term plans for integration. indeed there is a risk of rising racism and xenophobia. as the president said in his speech this morning, these have absolutely no place in the european union.
let me conclude with a subjective comment. since i'm chairing the meeting, i wasn't able to comment on the subject, but i did put my comments in writing, because i think this case is so important.
president of the council. - mr president, a few words to conclude this debate, which is certainly very interesting, and to say that it will have been useful if tomorrow it advances the question of roma integration. i think that is the main point to be made at the beginning of this afternoon and, as i said earlier, this question is regularly found in the conclusions of the various council configurations, including epsco, all of which are aimed at promoting the integration of the roma in the member states. the council's various texts show how often it rails against the stigmatization of an ethnic group. the belgian presidency has recently reiterated that integration remains one of the founding principles of the european union and that this issue deserves a debate in the appropriate places involving, of course, all the countries concerned with the necessary serenity. we have of course taken note of the recent decisions of the commission and the request to convene a joint epsco-jai council. that said, i shall not be speaking today on the advisability of this request. i would simply like to say that the epsco council has, at least in part, anticipated this request, since it has put the issue of roma integration on the agenda of its next meeting, which will be held on 21 october.
i have received six motions for resolutions tabled in accordance with rule 110 (2) of the rules of procedure. i'll close this discussion. the vote will take place on thursday 9 september 2010. the commission shall be assisted by the committee established by article 15 of regulation (eu) no 182/2011.
in writing. the events of the past few weeks in france have shown that simplifying the problem is not a lasting solution that will improve the situation of those affected. the roma constitute a special minority group, as they have dual minority status. they form an ethnic community and most of them belong to socially disadvantaged groups. today, roma are still victims of discrimination, marginalisation and segregation in many areas of public and private life. the roma community is still not recognised as a national or ethnic minority group in all member states and therefore does not enjoy the rights deriving from this status in all the countries concerned. consequently, their meaningful participation in public life is limited and in many cases can only take place on a voluntary basis. the social responsibility for the integration of roma communities is shared asymmetrically between the majority society and the roma. the majority society should accept and support the roma as a disadvantaged social group without assimilation. on the other hand, roma must accept the rules that govern society as a whole and take more initiative in dealing with their own problems. another lesson from events in france is that the social integration of roma communities cannot be organised within a purely national framework. in addition to local, regional and national levels, member states should cooperate within the eu framework. substantial resources should be allocated to this task from the union cohesion funds in the budgetary cycle after 2013.
in writing. we are witnessing daily sharp attacks on france's policy and harsh measures against the romanians and bulgarians. on the socialist and liberal side, some have branded president nicolas sarkozy as a populist, xenophobe and racist, exploiting the plight of the roma in western europe for their own partisan political ends. unfortunately, the issue of roma exiles has become too politicized. the unilateral emphasis on the principle of free movement within the european union tends to overlook the fact that the solution to the situation of the roma in eastern and central europe is not emigration or 'nomadisation' on a continental scale, but that their fate can be resolved in their own countries with the help of the member states and the eu. for the sake of propaganda, some forget that freedom of movement cannot be an end in itself, but the opposite: staying in one's own country and living in dignity is a fundamental universal and european value, to which europe's largest ethnic and social minority is entitled. we must therefore strive to make every citizen of the union feel at home in his own country and thus avoid having to seek his fortune in foreign countries. to the pro-democratic gypsy defenders who are pushing the boundaries of political cynicism, perhaps the most direct answer is the words of nobel peace prize winner elie wiesel: after all, gypsies are not sent to auschwitz, they are sent to romania.
the next item is the statement by the commission on the humanitarian situation following the floods in pakistan.

my fellow members of the house of representatives! the global expansion and internationalisation of gas trade has brought with it the risk that international political and economic conflicts could have a direct or indirect impact on the integrated gas market, jeopardising its functioning and thus security of supply. any gas market model is predominantly able to address only the shorter-term capacity, supply, engineering and technological and price change problems and to approximate the overall cost of the service to a minimum. therefore, security of supply should be defined as a guarantee that the required quantity of gas is continuously available to uninterrupted customers at an affordable price. in the development of security of supply policy, particular attention should be paid to temperature-dependent household consumers who are not able to switch energy. these consumers are not in a position to fight for their own security of gas supply. in the now fully liberalised gas market, the state, its government, the regulatory authorities have an obligation to include all acceptable service requirements in the general service contract.
i agree with those who say that the gas regulation will help us to protect ourselves more effectively against supply disruptions, and i thank the rapporteur for that. i consider the provisions on consumer protection to be particularly important. i am pleased that instead of the initial narrow definition, district heating networks and small businesses will also be given priority protection. this is not a small number of consumers, since 600 000 households in hungary alone are served by district heating networks. the protection of small businesses is important because, as we saw in the first days of 2009, these companies could easily be close to bankruptcy in the event of a gas supply disruption. my fellow members of the house of representatives! at the same time, let me remind you that while the adoption of the gas supply regulation is a significant step, it cannot replace energy investments. last week, four new member states sent a letter to commissioner oettinger, calling on the visegrad countries to strengthen the eu's financial instruments to support energy investment, in particular for the next seven-year financial plan. we can only agree with what is in this letter. the eu's efforts to develop alternative transport routes could provide a reassuring picture of gas supply. and i think that this intention should finally be reflected in the budget figures.
it is important that the european union responds at community level to energy security, but member states that have not yet adequately resolved their bilateral political relations with russia must also do so. 80% of hungary's natural gas imports come from russia, which makes hungarian consumers extremely dependent, and residents using district heating are particularly vulnerable. hungary therefore clearly wants to diversify both its sources of supply and its transport routes. we have clearly supported and will support both the nabucco and the south stream projects and the use of eu community funds. but the cheapest way for us to get through ukraine is to have a stable, reassuring transit arrangement. we ask the commission to act at community level, to mediate between russia and ukraine and to enforce a guarantee system for ukrainian transit.

in writing. it must be admitted that, even before the new lisbon requirements, annual budgets could only be adopted by mobilising exceptional financial instruments in the last four years of the current financial framework. it is therefore understandable that the revision of the current multiannual financial framework is now inevitable and that additional resources are needed for initiatives that could not have been foreseen when the current framework was adopted. the union's financial decision-makers have a duty to provide the means to achieve its objectives and policies, taking into account the new areas of action identified by the treaty of lisbon, including external action, sport, space, climate change, energy policy, tourism and civil protection. the european added value of the eu budget should be taken into account, as it is a manifestation of solidarity and efficiency through the collection and sharing of financial resources, dispersed at national, regional and local level, the vast majority of which supports long-term investments that stimulate the union's economic growth. the necessary steps should be taken to make available additional resources for the european external action service and other priorities related to the treaty of lisbon, as well as for other initiatives, in particular those under chapter 1a 'resilience for growth and jobs', which create union added value, allowing the union to fulfil its commitments and meet the expectations of its citizens.
the rapporteur. - my fellow members, commissioner, that is to say mr lewandowski and the council. the commission presented it, the council adopted the 7th amendment to the 2010 budget. what's the point of this? well, if we're gonna make a joke, that would be a good one. we create a blank line, a new line in the budget, in which we don't put billions, not a single cent. at the same time, journalists like to talk about 60 billion euros. this eur 60 billion, as the title suggests, is nothing more than a guarantee, the probability of which, in the commission's view, is zero. well, the worst-case scenario is that we're not always going to like it, so the bottom line is that there's a very small probability that you're actually going to have to move money through this line. why do you even care? and why now, in the 2010 budget? well, because what we're talking about is actually part of the plan that the european union has developed to solve the financial crisis of a country that's in trouble in the future. this is the element that primarily concerns the parliament and the budget of the european union, and it has a ceiling of no more than eur 60 billion for the period up to 2014. so this 60 billion is a fixed upper bound. why do we have to do this now? we have to do this because it's a message. a message to the market. a message to those who doubt that we want to save countries that are in trouble in the future. there's no need for it now, so there's no point in filling it with money, but it's worth waiting in line, because if there is, we'll be able to do it quickly. and the message from this empty line is also to the member states, who are worried about us taking more money from them, that this is not what is at issue. and this story is not about greece. it's about the future, first and foremost. it is therefore not right for a member state to avoid solidarity by invoking the mistakes of the greeks, whether they are perceived or not. this is the plan before us, the plan of solidarity between the member states, and without solidarity, ladies and gentlemen, there is no european union.

mr. president, i have to go. officer, please. the motion for a resolution rightly states that mountain regions, islands and sparsely populated areas face particularly difficult challenges and therefore require specific regional development programmes and support. the presentation is about gross domestic product, gdp, as an indicator. in this context, it is important to emphasise that gdp should continue to be used as a measure of member states' eligibility for regional policy support when allocating eu funds. gdp is a basic measure of development, development, the impact of regional development activities and an indicator that can be linked to other indicators. this does not mean, however, that the social and environmental consequences of economic decisions should be ignored, since only growth that is accompanied by social progress and an improvement in the quality of life is beneficial. at national level, decision-makers within individual countries may use alternative indicators to allocate development aid between regions, such as the allocation of member state aid to mountain, island and sparsely populated regions. however, i would like to draw the commission's attention to the fact that at eu level we must maintain gdp as the basic indicator for eligibility in order to continue to use the less developed member states effectively.
in writing. a controversial situation has been created by the international civil aviation organization. icao is strongly opposing the eu's efforts to extend eu ets standards to flights operated by non-european-based airlines. the inclusion of aviation in the ets is the right move, provided that it does not only affect eu airlines. the eu should therefore consistently enforce the principle of geographical neutrality, and environmental standards for european skies should also apply to flights from third countries. if strict standards were to apply only to european companies, this would give airlines from third countries an unjustified competitive advantage. the commission notes that the airport services agreement concluded between ryanair and ams was signed on the same day as the airport services agreement signed with ryanair on the same day. we must not forget the environmental consequences. with a partial regulation, we would probably have to continue to rely on voluntary offers from industry. and they haven't had the desired results. the much-touted fuel efficiency offers are worth little if they multiply as air traffic increases. it is a matter of record that icao estimates fuel consumption to quadruple by 2050. the eu must therefore take strong action to ensure that ets requirements apply to all flights in european skies. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's climate change strategy and will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's climate change strategy.

in writing. - my fellow members of parliament! i would like to thank commissioner günther oettinger for his efforts to assess whether eu legislation could prevent similar environmental disasters in the wake of the us rig accident. perhaps it can be concluded that community regulations focus on post-disaster action rather than prevention. this deficiency is given tragic relevance by a major environmental disaster. on monday, the dam of a tinplate plant in hungary broke. the mud, containing highly toxic chemicals, has flooded surrounding farmlands and communities. the event, which may have resulted in deaths, may have caused unforeseen pollution of soil and water. this is compounded by the fact that heavy metals can also be a serious air pollutant. the operator is responsible, but it is unlikely that it will be able to repair the damage alone. the lesson is clear. on the one hand, action at community level is needed to ensure that national authorities are consistently required to comply with legislation that appears to be too strict. secondly, the relevant regulation should be strengthened so that companies have adequate insurance in case of accidents of similar magnitude. until we make progress in these areas, the cost of the damage will be borne by the innocent taxpayers.

member of the commission. − madam president, first of all, after hearing everything that the honourable members of parliament have said, i should say thank you because i think that the messages and international nagoya line, about which you had more to say. but soon, after nagoya, we will also come with the proposal for the european union strategy on how to deal with this serious question and then of course many of the matters which you today underlined will have to be addressed. it will have to include a baseline, which we currently, finally, have. it will have to have measurable targets, not a lot but a few that are the closest to something we would like to achieve: the best proxies for what we would like to pursue. why a few? because this has to be fundamentally understood, so that we can share the understanding of biodiversity. we have done quite a lot in europe up to now. i am very cautious when we talk about implementation of natura 2000. i think that natura 2000 will still logging is a typical example of how we can really help, and finance even, the countries which are exporting timber, also in europe. i think it is extremely important that we understand that. as some this house today. the next question which i would like to mention is access and benefit-sharing (abs). for me, after sitting in new york discussing with practically all our partners, abs will be an important, if not the breakthrough, issue in nagoya. so we should do our utmost to ensure there is a breakthrough there. of course, you can make a difference, but it is not only about you and, after conventions. one was for climate change, the other was for biodiversity and there was also the separate issue of deforestation, an important one. increasingly they are separate and they are developing in parallel; we are increasingly understanding that we should start to reconnect them. many of the issues about mitigation and adaption in climate change have to do with biodiversity. red plus is as much for climate change as for dealing with biodiversity. so let us also prioritise the questions of biodiversity when we set the priorities on how we will use the money which is committed to red plus activities. and, when we talk about millennium development goals, it is the same story. i met and talked with helen clark in new york about how we can cooperate better between the commission and the undp in the future to ensure that these issues are more interconnected. the next issue which some of you underlined, and which i think is the basis of all the issues, is the integration of biodiversity, the mainstreaming of biodiversity in other policies. i fully share the view that if we want to talk about the cap, the fisheries policy, cohesion and other policies, we have to take that angle into account, too. when we talk about the common agricultural policy – which will soon be on your table, you will soon be discussing it – i think we should develop more the concept of the public simply too much at stake. we have responsibility, but i think it is fair to say others also have responsibility. but having parliament firmly behind us is really helpful.
acting president of the council. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank all those who have spoken to highlight the importance of biodiversity. that is a very important message that has been conveyed in this debate. i have already touched on many of the aspects mentioned in the introduction. i would like to go into some of the issues in more detail. first of all, the abs. the council is committed to finalising this protocol at cop10, which is important, because it will make a very important contribution to the achievement of all the objectives of the convention on biological diversity. the development and implementation of the abs protocol should be considered as an integral part of the process surrounding the convention on biological diversity. progress has already been made in this area in the recent talks in montreal, but it is true that a number of issues still need to be resolved before cop 10. this will require flexibility from all the negotiating partners, and thus also from within the eu. secondly, as regards the european parliament's motion for a resolution. this resolution addresses the key elements of biodiversity policy: the urgency of action, the economic aspects of ecosystems and biodiversity, the overall mission, vision, goals and indicators for the convention on biological diversity and its strategic plan, as well as the more specific aspects such as abs, synergies between the three rio conventions, etc. these elements are consistent with the positions being expressed within the council in view of the nagoya conference. the conclusions on this will normally be adopted by the environment council on 14 october. as regards the demand for the package and for sectoral integration, including in agriculture and fisheries, i would remind you that on 15 march the council specifically asked the commission to propose a package. i understand from the commissioner's words that this is being worked on very hard and that we can expect it. the council will be asked to adopt the conclusions on biodiversity at its meeting on 14 october, which is next week. these will then serve as a political guide for the negotiations in japan in october. the conclusions are intended to determine the main themes of cop10 and the council's position on them: the revised updated strategic plan of the convention on biological diversity, the negotiations for the protocol on access to genetic resources and the fair and equitable sharing of benefits arising from their utilization (abs), the strategy for the mobilisation of resources, including through innovative financing mechanisms, sectoral integration and synergies between the fight against climate change, desertification and policies to promote biodiversity. i want to thank everyone who has stressed the importance of this. i would also like to thank the commissioner for the good cooperation so far and also for the pleasant cooperation we may have in the run-up to and on the spot in nagoya.

mr president, commissioner, colleagues, i would like to thank you for your support. we welcome the fact that, following our resolution on serbia, montenegro and macedonia almost a year ago, we can now take a stand on the visa waiver for albania and bosnia and herzegovina. special recognition is therefore due to the hard work of the rapporteur, tanja fajon, and my colleague, anna maria corazza bildt. visa-free travel in europe is neither a privilege nor a diplomatic gesture. it is based on a mutual commitment that our political objectives cannot be jeopardised by security considerations. i would like to thank the commission and mrs malmström personally for the work they have done to make the visa-free regime for the western balkans sustainable. it gives a particular urgency to the question that we are a few days after the bosnian elections. the election results also show that deep divisions along ethnic lines cannot be resolved in one fell swoop. for the next generations, for young people, visa-free travel offers access to a european perspective, and this european perspective can help to promote reconciliation. that's why we need visa-free travel in this region. the way to create the conditions for living side by side is through the union, and by supporting visa-free travel we are contributing to this. and that is why we should not forget about kosovo, so we must also set a timetable for it as soon as possible.
in writing. 'the european parliament can once again demonstrate its support for the accession of the western balkan countries to the european union. i consider it very significant that, with the granting of visa-free travel to the citizens of albania and bosnia and herzegovina, almost all the inhabitants of the former yugoslavia will soon be able to enjoy the possibility of traveling without authorisation to the european union. neither the granting of visa-free travel nor membership is and cannot be subject to any conditions other than the fulfilment of the criteria. both countries have met the stringent requirements imposed on their domestic and judicial bodies and have demonstrated their willingness and ability to meet the diverse expectations. the on-the-spot checks also convinced the union experts that the security risks could be screened. it is unacceptable that governments' actions are influenced by domestic policy considerations rather than by an unbiased assessment of the fulfilment of the criteria. the granting of visa-free travel is also a signal to the countries concerned that they can overcome obstacles if they are prepared to do so by political agreement. i was pleased to note that in albania and bosnia and herzegovina the political forces were united in their efforts. i sincerely hope that the opportunity of eu membership in the form of tangible results like the present will accelerate their recovery and help to overcome the nationalist, divisive tendencies that are holding them back from joining. "
the division of germany was the most striking symbol of the absurdity and inhumanity of the bipolar world. without german reunification, there would have been no european reunification, and no enlargement in 2004 or 2007. there is no people in europe who has felt the artificial and absurd bifurcation of germany more than the hungarian people. the reunification of the german nation was the beginning of the reunification of europe. with the fall of the berlin wall, the walls of divided europe in the cold war collapsed. as a hungarian, i am particularly proud that the leaders of my country at that time, by a courageous and bold decision, allowed tens of thousands of east german citizens who had fled from the gdr to hungary to cross the iron curtain to austria. they broke the hungarian-gdr treaty, and thus broke the first brick of the berlin wall. gyula horn, then hungarian foreign minister and prime minister németh miklós, carried out a historic act that would not have been possible without mikhail gorbachev's reform efforts.
now that the various policies and directions decided on in the context of the 2020 strategy are being implemented in a more practical way, we must admit that we still know nothing about their financing or financial background. it is no coincidence that, at this time when we are stressing the importance and the need for cohesion policy, we also want to express our views in the budgetary debates that are about to begin on the fact that we are talking about a common european policy whose aim is to strengthen rather than to dismantle. there is no doubt, and this has been said by many, that one of the main priorities is to create new jobs, to increase employment, but we also know that there are structural tensions in europe's labour markets. where there are jobs, there are often no people with the right skills or the right skills, and elsewhere, well-educated people are unemployed because there are no jobs. the european social fund obviously plays a major role in this process. it is the task of these policies to balance these differences and bring them closer together. it's not about big projects. this is about smaller projects, more flexible adaptation. it is clear that while we ourselves accept and support the idea that a uniform policy is needed, that synergies need to be exploited in the field of cohesion policy, i would also like to stress that more flexibility, fewer rules and more transparency are needed in the area of the social fund. in this sense, therefore, the social fund should be subject to special rules, and i particularly welcome the commissioner's statement that he wants to introduce results-oriented evaluation, because the biggest problem very often is precisely that - and it erodes the credibility of these policies - if we do not see what results we achieve by spending money in certain directions.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. a strong and effective cohesion policy is, in my view, unthinkable. the political consensus needed to develop and implement this, if you will, without political cohesion, is what i believe the political consensus between the political groups, the political families and perhaps beyond that, between the various european institutions, is aimed at. in this respect, i find unacceptable, i consider unacceptable, the discrepancies that have been made in today's debate about gdp as the number one exclusive indicator of eligibility for regional development aid. i would like to support, in the strongest possible terms, the strong position of mr olbrycht and mrs mazzoni. the commissioner shared my strong view, which i had already expressed in previous parliamentary debates, that gdp may not reflect all the dimensions of development, but it is the most reliable indicator currently known. to make up for any shortcomings and to fully reflect environmental and social considerations at member state level, decision-makers in individual countries can of course turn to alternative indicators, but it is essential for european solidarity to prevail and for member states to catch up effectively that gdp remains the fundamental and exclusive measure of the distribution of resources at eu level.
dear andor and hahn! cohesion policy is the most successful and spectacular public policy of the european union. it is an instrument that the union will need very much in the future. the catch-up of the new member states in central europe and the baltic is far from over, but certain regions in spain, portugal and greece need these resources. but it would be a mistake to think that cohesion policy is just a catch-up policy, as cohesion policy is the most comprehensive integrated investment policy that helps to achieve the objectives of the 2020 strategy. it would be a huge mistake to tear this cohesion policy apart. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the new member states. and i think it is very important, as a member of the committee on agriculture, that there should be much greater coherence and synergy between regional policy and rural development policy in the future.
, in writing. – during its history the esf has always adapted to the current economic priorities of the eu. it is evident that the esf has to adapt itself again, because circumstances have changed. it has to be adapted to the eu2020 strategy, where raising employment is a key goal. it has to adapt to the crisis. the esf is not a crisis management tool per se, but now it has to function during a period of crisis. we should not forget that, even when macroeconomic crises are over, employment levels may remain low. unemployment has risen in europe. some european countries are forced to introduce budget cuts, which threaten jobs, especially in the public sector. the majority of public workers are women, therefore public sector lay-offs threaten women in the first instance. enhancing women’s labour market participation is a key goal for eu2020 and for all reasonable political decision makers – women’s participation is particularly important in order to protect and reshape our social model. therefore we should help those who lose their jobs due to budget cuts. some countries would favour the esf being nationalised to some extent in the next budget. but i strongly believe that the european social fund has to remain an integral part of eu policies.
in writing. when talking about the future of cohesion policy, and in particular its objectives, priorities and structure, it is essential to mention the strategic infrastructure network across the union, which promotes the integration of eu and regional energy markets and the use of renewable energy sources. a very important proportion of eu energy investments is in urban and rural development projects financed by the structural funds. this includes, inter alia, investments in district heating, individual energy efficiency projects, and projects for the development of local and regional energy networks. the use of existing union funds for energy efficiency measures should be improved in the future; and a greater use of structural funds and cohesion fund resources for this purpose should be promoted. the resources for research, development and demonstration in the field of energy should be doubled by 2020 compared to current levels, including a significant increase in the future eu budget allocation for renewable energy, smart grids and energy efficiency in particular, as one of the means to achieve this could be the european union's cohesion and regional policy post-2013.
in writing. the draft empl resolution rightly highlights the need to support smes and the key to creating synergies between the structural funds, as the esf's success story is not without its many flaws and problems, especially in the context of the financial and economic crisis. moreover, the positive potential and impact of the european economic recovery plan, which previously allowed temporary employment of workers with disabilities under the rules of the general block exemption regulation (ec) no 800/2008 to be granted by member states with additional subsidies and compensation subject to a number of conditions, will be reduced in the future. however, the proposed simplification of the esf should not focus primarily on issues related to the management of the funds, but on the actual synergy (cross-financing, accountability) in the planning and implementation by the member states. in addition, efforts should be made to reduce the number of experts without formal qualifications, as unemployment among disadvantaged groups should be reduced. at the same time, expert networks and professional cooperation should be strengthened, and this could include the development of cross-border cooperation, taking into account intra-regional links. last but not least, in response to commissioners hahn and andor, there is also a need to focus more on the issues of resilience and sustainability: reducing the burden on families, reducing the disparities in the social division of labour that are harmful to childbearing, improving accessibility are essential to achieving and ensuring a more active, healthier age.

budget, we asked ourselves how and when we would fund the foreign service. and the answer was, you don't need a resource, because you can do it by redistributing. well, we on the budget committee didn't really believe that. the truth is somewhere in between. the majority of the costs could have been covered by a transfer, but the addition of the two amendments would have resulted in an additional cost of approximately eur 44 million. this additional cost covers about a quarter of the expenditure, and at the same time we have seen that the set-up of the service is still at 10%. obviously, the money that i hope will be voted on tomorrow would cover a little bit more time than it's actually being spent. this would have given rise to, could have given rise to, tasks, such as adjusting the amount. but we decided that it didn't make sense now. it is much more important that this service be set up as soon as possible, that it go forward, because one of the important messages of the lisbon treaty is that we are well visible in the world. at the same time, when we have avoided any delaying action, we have set out expectations, as previous rapporteurs have done. we think geographical proportionality is very important in the setup of the service, but we didn't put in mandatory reserve training, again just to get this whole system going. we also want to make sure that the rapporteur is the committee on budgetary control. − on behalf of the committee on budgetary control, i welcome the establishment of the new body, which is one of the most important practical transpositions of the lisbon treaty. the new body needs a budget, of course, but it is equally important to define and comply with budgetary principles, financial rules, because of budgetary and financial accountability. in recent years, our committee has had a debate with dg relex on this, and i would like to emphasise this in particular. it is very important to establish the appropriate organisational and operational rules. it is important to avoid duplication of tasks, functions and resources and to take into account all possibilities for ensuring the effectiveness of expenditure. the new organisation will be effective only if it has a clear and simple organisational structure and resource management. the staff will be drawn from three sources: the eu institutions, the member states and experts from the host country. it is particularly important that the institutions and staff of the member states should be fully equal in this respect, which is why we consider training to be important and it is very important that the selection principle and practice be based on uniform processes and requirements. the committee on budgets will check this annually.
in writing. in the midst of the global economic crisis, in the european year against poverty and social exclusion three days after world food day, it is particularly important that child poverty is given due attention at european level. every six seconds, a child dies of hunger on earth. millions of malnourished children live in europe. the proportion of children living in poverty, in many cases in a cumulative disadvantaged situation, is 19% at eu level, 3% higher than the proportion of the total population. the high level of attention is also supported by the figures and should be given due weight in the eu-wide strategy on children's rights. the current global economic crisis is not only a burden but also an opportunity. it's an opportunity for creative thinking, for a paradigm shift in business, for a paradigm shift in our economic, social, environmental thinking, which is becoming more and more open in our societies. with community engagement, with local initiatives, we can help to reduce child poverty, child malnutrition. in hungary, the recently launched 'every child lives well! initiative' is bringing hot meals to thousands of children every day. while thanks to the initiative launched by the franciscan monk böjte csaba in transylvania, it is possible to provide for the feeding and supervision of nearly 2000 children in 18 boarding houses and 40 day-care centers. i would also like to stress, in agreement with the opinion of the international organisation eurochild, that the fight against child poverty must be seen not only as an economic challenge but also as a question of children's rights.
in writing. – when talking about poverty, we often forget those who are seriously underpaid in their jobs. in this regard, poverty still has a feminine face in europe. the disadvantageous situation of women is structural. they work less, as household work is not acknowledged as legitimate work. if they work on the labour market, they earn less, because of discrimination and segregation. a reminder: all in all, the gender pay gap is still over 17% in europe (meaning that women work 17% more for the same pay men get). and in the end, of course, women also get less pension due to the circumstances described above. is this not profoundly unfair? we can break this vicious cycle by acknowledging that non-paid work is work indeed, by encouraging women to work and gain their economic independence, by attacking the structural causes of labour market segregation, and by paying attention to women's problems when reforming our pension schemes. it may be more expensive today, but in the long run less inequality and less poverty will help balance our budgets as well. tackling women's poverty would be beneficial for all of us - not only for women, but also for men.
in writing. other with regard to the debate on the minimum income, it is important to emphasize that we must not decide about today, tomorrow, but the day after tomorrow. inactivity is more destructive than many of us even realize, you know. but for people with disabilities, it's a daily experience. we don't need to have a single-digit percentage increase in income as a jobless person. we need to increase activity, help at all costs. in addition, research in gerontology and lifestyle should be supported. the union's contribution for the period 2014-2020 is estimated at eur 10000000000. not legalizing addiction, but strengthening it by increasing the masses of dependent and needy citizens. it's also a self-reinforcing stigma that's unsustainable! social benefits are increasingly less able to be generated from public resources (taxes). people with severe disabilities who are in real need, who are so-called helpless, are getting worse and worse: we see more and more examples, for example in the field of invalidity pensions, of widespread abuse, which has led to a deterioration in the quality of care. returning to ageing, the subsistence rate is continuing to rise, and the value of existing social benefits will decline, as there will not be enough workers - the number of workers in europe will continue to decline by the order of millions over the next decade - to maintain social and minimum income levels. this is a prediction of a deterioration in the lot of those who are actually in need, which is unacceptable!
