mr president, the 2011 eu budget is the first to be prepared under the lisbon treaty, but it is not the first in the age of austerity. the global financial crisis has revealed the deep structural weaknesses of the european development model, based on high taxes, high fiscal redistribution, inflexible labour markets and unnecessarily extended welfare provisions. now it is time to rethink and restructure fiscality, not only at the national level but also at the european one. the 2011 eu budget shows some signs of austerity but does not go far enough in structural change. this is related to the fact that many people think that quantity is quality, namely the bigger the spending, the better its impact on the european economy and society. moreover, they demand additional funds because the lisbon treaty and the europe 2020 strategy imposed new mandates on the community. but there is a fundamental misunderstanding in this concept. the eu budget is small compared to national ones – and rightfully so. it does not need to grow in order to perform its functions. what matters most is the structure of the budget and the effectiveness of spending. sharp focus on innovation, r&d, higher education, selected areas of energy transport, agriculture and rural development will do. our budget is completely dissimilar to national budgets, which have very little discretionary spending. we do have a lot of discretion. the eu budget should be regarded as a capital budget, rather than a current one. it should focus only on investments in the common european future. that is why our group feels strongly that the eu budget does not need to grow. it never substitutes the national budgets representing national sovereignty. there is no need for an eu-wide tax. taxes at the eu level would not substitute, but inevitably supplement, national taxation, increasing further the tax disadvantages of the european area compared to the rest of the world. the ecr says no to bond issuance as well. debt securities imply debt and would open the door to deficits building up debt. we have enough debt at the national level and even more unfunded liabilities. we do not need additional layers of debt which would destroy the future of european youth and make europe’s terminal decline irreversible. the ecr agrees with the priorities of the 2011 budget as stated by the rapporteur – youth, education and mobility – but, if we take these goals seriously, it logically follows that what our young people need is definitely not a greater fiscal burden in the age of adverse demographics, but more solidarity and investment in our common future.

madam president, i would like to thank you for your kind words. my fellow members of parliament! in view of the constraints that are also affecting and holding back the hungarian economy, it is certainly welcome that the regulation promotes the consolidation of payment discipline and the predictability of financial processes, thus contributing to improving the situation of financial operators. the amendments tabled, on the one hand, clarify and thereby improve the original proposal, and, on the other hand, in contrast to the original objectives, weaken the deterrent forces contained in the original proposal, particularly on the part of the authorities. in particular, derogation from the 30 days is not allowed for public authorities. background controls shall be applied to ensure that this time limit is respected. the same treatment should be accorded to undertakings and authorities as regards penalties. the basic objective would be immediate payment in order to stimulate the economy and to promote the financial stability of small and medium-sized enterprises. in contrast, there is no provision in the proposal to promote this. large international multinationals, abusing their significant market share and dominance, impose contracts on their domestic partners, often with 90- or 120-day deadlines and possible moral sanctions for failure to comply. however, even if the supplier in a vulnerable position has a clear legal status, he does not dare to apply the penalties, since he would then be excluded from the orders. in order to avoid this, it should be possible to legislate in all cases for the compulsory clearance of costs arising from late payment, with penalties even for the creditor entrepreneur in the event of non-clearance. thank you so much for listening.

on behalf of the s&d group. first of all, i would like to thank you and all the european citizens who have expressed their sympathy with the disaster in hungary. the tragic event in hungary is also a loss for europe. the aim of today's debate is to turn the eu's solidarity intention into concrete help and to think through the prevention of future environmental disasters. my fellow citizens! i propose that we restructure the solidarity fund. it is appropriate to extend the scope of aid to industrial disasters and to reduce the threshold for the financial damage. this is all the more justified because mrs georgieva told us that we have used only 10% of this fund in the last 10 years. today's debate should also address five far-reaching environmental safety issues. first time around. all reservoirs, whether still in operation or abandoned, are subject to technical and environmental review in accordance with uniformly established eu principles. the jointly agreed technical discipline should be respected by the member states and monitored by the european commission. second of all. industrial units that do not properly store red sludge should be classified as hazardous industrial plants. third time. accelerate the implementation of the mining waste directive in the member states. fourth time. i also proposed in a written question that research programmes on the recycling of red mud should be supported by the union. for the fifth time. during the hungarian eu presidency, the industrial and mining waste dumps in the danube catchment area should be repaired and recultivated under the danube strategy. finally, the only industrial activity in 21st century europe should be one which either makes the waste it produces harmless or recycles it. this will make europe safer and the natural environment more livable.
on behalf of the ecr group. in the last two weeks, the press in hungary has been talking about almost nothing but the responsibility of the company, the hungarian aluminium industry joint stock company. the ceo was arrested before the investigation even started, let alone had a chance to lead to a result. the investigation will determine the company's responsibility, but i think we can take a lesson from it. it is not only the company that has a responsibility, but also the authorities and the municipalities. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. in hungary, the public administration, the central government administration, but also the local governments are not sufficiently prepared to prevent such disaster situations. the administration is extremely fragmented, it's unfunded, and it doesn't have the human or financial resources to prevent these things. i suggest that we draw lessons from this and take it into account in the context of municipal and administrative reform.

in writing. although the hungarian prime minister said that hungary is a strong enough country to cope with the consequences of such a disaster on its own, i would like to thank the european commission and the member states for the assistance provided and offered so far to cope with the consequences of the disaster. the hungarian disaster also demonstrates the need for stricter community legislation in the field of civil protection. the lisbon treaty provides for this, making disaster prevention a shared responsibility of the community and the member states. the ajka disaster may be the first real test of the environmental liability directive, which came into force in 2007, and will show whether the polluter pays principle can be fully implemented. the legislation, among other things, proposes that companies create a financial reserve to deal with the consequences of possible industrial disasters. i think it is worth considering whether to make it mandatory to set aside such a fund for companies dealing with hazardous materials. i urge the hungarian government to enter into negotiations with the european commission as soon as possible on the transfer of resources for environmental protection and rural development to disaster relief. i welcome the proposal made by the european commission in its communication on the budgetary review published on tuesday to make the use of the solidarity fund available for the future to help deal with the consequences of industrial disasters.

as in the great debate on poverty in the european parliament last week, the politicians of the european union are now surprised and saddened to see that the situation of the people of europe is not getting better, but getting worse. poverty, misery, unemployment, homelessness are on the rise. and they wonder about it as if it's at least the result of some unfortunate natural disaster, tsunami, something. no, it's the consequence of the decisions of european politicians. the increase in poverty and misery is a direct result of the neoliberal policies that you have pursued, with the exception of those who have opposed them, and this trend will continue. there will be more misery, more poverty, if you don't choose another set of values. these two reports are like a medical board finding that the therapy that they've been doing with the patient has been harming the patient, making the patient worse, and then as a post-diagnosis therapy, they're saying that we should continue doing what we've been doing, just with a little bit more rigorous monitoring, and punishing the patient if they don't take their medication, for example. it's a conscious choice between values. the european union has always consciously chosen the value system of always asking what is the right decision for money and profit, what is the right decision for multinational companies and banks, and never asking what is the right decision for people and society. he always asked what was good in terms of unlimited fairness and unrestricted competition from a moral standpoint, and never what was good in terms of social justice and solidarity. we need to change that and make good decisions now.

in writing. − to those of us from the new member states, the omission of any legally binding commitment to geographic balance in the european external action service is a disappointment. it is true that there have been several political statements promising that the interests of the new member states will be taken into account. however positive a political commitment might be, the absence of a legal provision is regrettable. without legal provision, it is hard to see how voters in the new member states will feel that they own the service. this is why a number of us have had misgivings about giving our full support to the rapkay report.
the rapporteur. - mr president, colleagues, i have to say that i am very sorry. officer and deputy ashton! the european instrument for democracy and human rights is the financial instrument supporting human rights, the rule of law, the protection of democracy and conflict prevention worldwide. the beneficiaries of this financial instrument are mainly non-governmental organisations, non-governmental organisations, individuals fighting for human rights in the most vulnerable situations in third countries. the main advantage of this financial instrument, unlike other geographical instruments, is that the agreement of the government of the beneficiary country is not required for the granting of the aid. so this is a very important role. however, the cost of this instrument, which is linked to the general vat burden, has not been financed by the community budget so far, and the european commission has therefore proposed a technical amendment which would have facilitated the operation and activities of ngos and non-governmental organisations in third countries from this budget. because these organizations are key to the promotion of human rights in these countries, to the establishment of political pluralism, it is very important that we do not make it difficult for organizations that are already in difficulty. we therefore welcome this initiative of the commission and, in agreement with my fellow rapporteur, mrs lochbihler, we fully support it. but that's only one side of the coin. on the other side of the coin is the political significance of the case. the issue of the financial instrument has become part of an interinstitutional struggle that has been going on for a year. the human rights financing instrument is essential for ngos, and it is therefore important that the european parliament be involved in the development of strategic and multiannual framework programmes. we are fighting for the european parliament to have a say in the development of the financial instrument's strategic plans, if it deems it necessary, and to do so by monitoring it annually, not just every seven years, during the financial cycles. that's what the amendments are about, and that's what we're trying to back up with these. the treaty of lisbon has confirmed the right of democratic control of the european parliament, which is in fact the institution of the 'delegated act', as my colleagues have already mentioned, which we consider necessary to promote and apply in the present situation. this case is precisely the first example of the european parliament's role actually increasing after 1 december 2009. after the negotiations and parliamentary consultations that have taken place so far, we have decided together, as rapporteurs for financial instruments, to send the dossier to second reading and to send a strong political message to the institutions, since we must act in the spirit of the treaty of lisbon in the period after it. we believe that these are the instruments that are most needed to enable parliament to exercise its right of democratic control.
the rapporteur. − i thank my colleagues for participating in this debate and for having drawn attention to the same issue that we, the rapporteurs, have been facing in recent times. thank you, commissioner, for your constructive approach to this debate. what we have seen today - and almost all the speakers have confirmed this - is that the european financial instrument for democracy and human rights is very important and perhaps the only instrument of the union which goes to the democratisation of third countries, to the protection of human rights, at a level that can be received directly by ngos. organisations operating at local level can receive it without the need for the support of the third country concerned. it is therefore important, and we consider it very necessary, to facilitate all the technical facilities that will enable these organizations to access their very often vital funding as quickly as possible. at the same time, precisely because it is in such a high priority area that we are talking about this instrument, which is linked to ngos and civil society organisations, it is important that parliament has a say in what strategy, what long-term programme, it is a key issue that parliament sees how these funds are used, and not just at the end of seven-year time frames. we therefore consider it important to emphasise the right of democratic control and participation of parliament. i believe in finding a common solution, especially after this morning's meeting with both high representative ashton and commissioner piebalgs, and i also believe that if there is political will, it will overcome legal obstacles. so that's how we're gonna look at the next few weeks.
mr president, developments in ukraine clearly illustrate the old saying about how power corrupts and absolute power corrupts absolutely. the present ruling élite has been exercising power since the election of president yanukovych and much of what has been done points in the direction of favouring the concentration of power. this is all the more striking if we recall the results of the presidential elections which gave yanukovych a small majority and demonstrated that he had strong support in the south and east of the country only. yet – and this is the striking feature of ukrainian politics currently – there has been a steady movement in the direction of marginalising alternative centres of power. the media, rule of law, the opposition, the autonomy of the state administration have all been affected. what is particularly worrying is that the secret police have acquired the capacity to intervene very extensively in the affairs of the country. all this augurs badly for the future, especially if ukrainians genuinely seek a european future, because the system being built by yanukovych points away from europe, not towards it. the most that can be said for the changes is that they have brought a degree of stability to the country, but increasingly this stability foreshadows stasis and immobility. at the same time, it is true, yanukovych has certainly improved relations with russia, but has thereby accepted a degree of subordination to moscow that is new. whether ukrainian society will accept this transformation in the longer term is an open question.

the following is the commission statement on the anti-counterfeiting trade agreement (acta).
member of the commission. − mr president, first of all there have been several interventions claiming that the implementation of acta would lead to limiting civil liberties, and several pointed out the control of laptops, or of air passengers at borders for example. the joint declaration of 16 april issued by all the acta parties is quite clear. there is no proposal to force acta participants to require border authorities to search travellers’ baggage or their personal electronic devices for infringing materials. acta is about tackling large-scale illegal activity and pursuing criminal organisations. it is not about limiting civil liberties or harassing consumers. acta will be in line with the current eu regime for enforcement of iprs, which fully respect fundamental rights and freedoms and civil liberties, such as the protection of personal data. one example is the di minimis clause in the 2003 eu customs regulation that exempts travellers from checks if the infringing goods are not part of large-scale traffic. eu customs, frequently confronted with drug trafficking, weapons or people, have neither the time nor the legal basis to look for a couple of pirated songs on an ipod or laptop computer, and we have no intention of changing this. we will not change this, and we will ensure that acta parties can continue to apply such an exemption. however, we cannot impose the de minimis exemption as an absolute obligation, because some member states acting under national rules have kept the authority to carry out certain controls of passengers. reference has been made again to the three-strike law or the hadopi law in france which makes this possible. however, this is a national rule and the european union does not have the authority to make a national state change this. several have also been asking for an impact assessment on fundamental rights, an impact study on privacy and an impact study on the acquis communautaire. i have made several statements in this plenary that there has been no infringement whatsoever of fundamental rights or the acquis communautaire in any way, and i must say that in the three debates that we have already had in this plenary none of you has given an example of problems related to fundamental liberties. nobody has pointed to an infringement of the acquis communautaire. nobody has been able to give an example. if you give us examples, we will look into them. as for negotiating behind closed doors, parliament has been asking for more transparency on the acta negotiations. it is the commission that has made sure that this happens; that you have the text before you that was negotiated in tokyo and that you got it a couple of days after it was finalised. you have it also with the reserves that still exist; three on the part of the european union, three on the part of the united states. you have the result of the negotiations. how can you say that this has happened behind closed doors? the question has been put to the ombudsman. maybe you do not trust me. i am the commission. maybe you do not trust the council. maybe you do not trust your governments. i have detected from a lot of speeches here that your trust in national governments is not very deep, but i think you at least trust the ombudsman. the ombudsman has said very clearly that we can keep documents confidential, and what we have been doing is making a large additional step in the direction of transparency. you would expect that when you do that, you would be applauded. at least there would be some consideration for the fact, but some of you simply continue to say that we negotiate behind closed doors and that there is no transparency. so what is the use in making an effort to have more transparency if afterwards you still continue to say that there is no transparency? maybe i would be better investing my time in something else. as regards the possibility of the european commission agreeing on a final text: in the lisbon treaty, there are in fact very clear rules on how international agreements are negotiated, by whom and how they are concluded and ratified, including the important role of the european parliament. there are also clear rules in the framework agreement as to how parliament must be informed and heard during the negotiating process. i think we have respected these rules scrupulously. it is the commission’s prerogative, as a negotiator, to determine the point at which negotiations are technically finalised and at which the agreement can be initialled. the agreement is not yet initialled and you have the possibility tonight to make your remarks before we even do the initialling and before we even decide to initial the agreement as such. we have not yet made a decision in the commission on what we are actually going to do, because we still have a couple of reserves that we want to settle with the united states before making up our minds. the initialling of the agreement is part of the commission’s prerogatives and it does not definitely bind the union. the agreement will become definitive once the european parliament has given its consent. so let us respect the treaty and let us respect the framework agreements. in the meantime we will continue to inform and engage with you as stipulated in the framework agreement. by the way, according to the treaty, it is the council which authorises the signature of the agreements, but parliament always has the last word. it has to ratify this agreement and if consent is refused, there is simply no agreement at all. so be a little bit patient. there is no initialling yet. once there is initialling, you have the translation, you have the check by the lawyer-linguists, and then it comes to the council for signature and to parliament for ratification. so no decision whatsoever has been taken at this moment in time, and you have another possibility tonight to make known all the comments that you have on your mind. some have also been asking how the eu would benefit by entering into such an agreement if it will go no further than its current laws and if, furthermore, other countries like the us also claim that it will not change domestic law. this is not about substantive law. this agreement is about the enforcement of existing law, and that is why i have repeatedly stipulated that we are not going to change the acquis communautaire. the acquis communautaire is about substantive law and we are not changing that. an international treaty that would adopt standards similar to those of the eu, but also to those already in place in countries like the us or japan, would still be a most valuable contribution to the current prevailing international standard as defined by the wto trips agreement. our goal is to promote acta standards to key emerging partners, namely through our future trade agreements but also in multilateral venues. that being said, several acta parties have taken the momentum created by the acta negotiations to revise their domestic legislation along the agreed lines. as far as i am aware, for instance, both japan and canada are in the process of reviewing their internet enforcement regimes. moreover, it is often overlooked that acta is not only about improved legal standards. it is also about cooperation between enforcement authorities, the adoption of best practices or the better coordination of technical assistance. although the eu has had very successful cooperation with the us in these areas for the last four to five years, we believe that acta can also improve those important aspects of the fight against ipr infringements. we consider that the us enforcement system is generally effective and efficient in the protection of certain intellectual property rights. the commission has stressed that acta is not a disguised means to circumvent their domestic legislative process and to devise their current laws and so has parliament. it is understandable that us officials stress the same line. let me add that the principle of cooperation between rightholders is already in article 15 of the e-commerce directive since 2003, so this is not a new concept. it is in the e-commerce directive and we are simply referring to that. we are referring to the existing eu law. let me further say that what is often overlooked in the debate on acta are the number of jobs of eu citizens that relate to intellectual property rights and we often discuss jobs on all sides of the house. millions of jobs in europe depend on respect for intellectual property rights. counterfeiting is a serious attack on european industry and on the european economy and on european innovation, because we are an innovation-based economy. what we are really talking about is jobs. and i must say that i am a little bit surprised that in the three discussions that we have now had in this plenary, this has never in fact been mentioned. this reference to jobs has never been made. the references that are made are to fundamental rights and i am very sensitive to that, but without any confirmative example. the reference is made to controls at the borders where it is clear that acta adds nothing to what already exists. there is a reference to privacy and i see no reason why privacy would be under attack. there are references to a kind of nebulous liberty and liberties that you think are under attack, but you do not give any examples of that. on the other hand, what is very clear and what is documented in all kinds of impact studies that have already been done by the way by the commission, on all kinds of items, is that a lot of our jobs are linked to intellectual property rights and that is one of the reasons we attach such importance to that topic. i am a little bit surprised that this is very rarely mentioned by parliament.

in writing. is the eu really capable of receiving intelligence from the us? the motion for a resolution on passenger name record (pnr) is welcome as it seeks to promote an agreement between the united states of america and the european union that balances security and data protection. it left me with a sense of loss. i agree with mrs reding, who stresses the culture of reciprocity, and i ask the question: if we really believe that it is important to exchange information reciprocally, that is, not only to give but also to receive information from the united states that is relevant to counter-terrorism, then it is not enough to simply submit a wish list to the united states, but in practice we must also create or appoint an eu body that is able to receive and process intelligence from the united states and at the same time provide uniform access to information for the member states. this problem was already raised in connection with the swift convention, which records the transfer of bank transaction data, but has not been resolved since. it would have been fortunate if the motion had also given a strong impetus to this imminent task.
member of the commission. - mr president, thank you for your message. i shall try, without ambition, to answer each one precisely; we'll have plenty of chances to meet again. i will begin my answer by quoting the first speaker, mr andreas schwab, who recalled the initial ambition we must reinvigorate, to which we must remain faithful, namely the social market economy. he added, as i think we all like these three words: social market economy; it is important and naturally the lisbon treaty uses these three words, adding the ambition of very strong competitiveness. it is precisely this, ladies and gentlemen, that is the ambition behind the single market act, a highly competitive social market economy, and to achieve this, our conviction, and mine in particular, is that - i repeat - for competitiveness and growth, every citizen is needed, every company is needed, every community is needed. it can't fall from the sky; even though we have laws to build, frameworks to build, we need citizens, businesses, communities to take ownership of the opportunities and opportunities of this big market. that's the ambition. and naturally, i say to mr schwab, as i say to many of you, that at the end of this debate we will certainly have to set priorities that are bigger or more precise than those we have been able to set ourselves, even though we have already identified 29 or 30 of the top 50 priorities. i would remind you that there were many more ideas in the report by mario monti. but i would also like to agree with what mr creutzmann or mrs corazza bildt have said, that we must be aware, in a way echoing my analysis at the beginning of my speech, of what is happening in many of our countries as a result of the crisis, as a result of misunderstanding, as a result of the question 'what does the market bring us?' to which the answer is 'not much'. this creates a feeling of fear, of fear, of incomprehension, and behind all this we see the rise of populism and protectionism. the first victim of protectionism would be the single market. that is why we must, through action, dynamism and initiatives, oppose these attempts or temptations to protectionism. president harbour said that this was a new commitment. in my hearing, mr president, on 13 january, i remember specifically saying that during the five years we were going to spend together, i would try to reconcile with other colleagues - because i am not the only commissioner, there are probably fifteen or sixteen commissioners to inherit a greater or lesser share of the legislation - 1 500 texts that have to be lived and lived intelligently on the market. 1,500 words! but i said, mr president harbour, that i intended to work towards reconciling citizens and businesses with this large market, and you are right to stress the chance we can give ourselves with this debate and with proposals from the commission on a global or holistic action plan, to use the encouraging words of louis grech. mr harbour spoke of the growth of competitiveness. you will find in this single market act many concrete proposals that we will be working on in the legal and tax environment for companies and, in particular, for the smallest ones, the revision of the accounting guidelines in 2011, a common consolidated corporate tax base - my colleague semeta is working on this - new strategies for vat, the interconnection of company registers. these are a number of concrete proposals to facilitate the regulatory, fiscal and financial environment for small and mediumsized enterprises, and to these should be added the digital agenda on which i am working with my colleague nellie kroes, who is working very hard. retail trade and many other ideas that mrs handzlik mentioned earlier. i would like to say a few words about the social dimension, which i am also interested in. mr triantaphyllides, mr hughes, mrs gebhardt and mrs turunen, you mentioned them at the very beginning of your discussion. we have had discussions on this issue, mrs turunen, and we have taken positions which are not necessarily those which you read in the newspapers. i think that such a debate within the college is normal. the opposite would be abnormal. we are 27 people with different sensibilities, traditions and nationalities, and by working together we are in solidarity with these proposals. we are working to find the centre of gravity of the general european interest for proposals which are then discussed here in parliament and in the council of ministers, to be completed or improved. we have therefore had discussions, and this is normal, to find the right balance between the economic freedoms of the treaty, free movement and the legitimate freedoms and rights of the social partners, particularly in the field of collective action. i think it is legitimate for employees as well as companies to have a clear and safe framework in which to work. this is the spirit which will guide the commission's action. we will ensure, on the basis of the new strategy for the implementation of the charter of fundamental rights, that the rights guaranteed by the charter, including the right to take collective action, are taken into account. the commission will implement the horizontal social clause in article 9 of the treaty to the letter by carrying out a thorough prior analysis of the social impact of all these legislative proposals on the single market. in any case, i remain in favour of inserting, where necessary, a provision specifying how collective rights will be guaranteed in the context of the new legislation. in other words, the need for such a clause will have to be assessed on a case-by-case basis. this is something we will be discussing with you in the coming months, if possible to improve the commission's proposals further. this will also be the case, mrs vergnaud, mrs auconie, of this quality framework to which president barroso has committed himself on services of general thank you for your time, mr. president. my fellow members of parliament! the visa system is an effective tool to prevent illegal entry and residence in the european union. when deciding which third country nationals are subject to visa requirements, we need to look at the situation of each country individually, taking into account illegal immigration, public policy and security issues, the european union's external relations, coherence within the region and, of course, the principle of reciprocity. the evaluation mechanism should operate in such a way that for all countries which have made a step back from the abovementioned criteria or which have introduced a visa requirement for nationals of one or more member states, the visa requirement should be reinstated immediately. the immigration and security implications of the lifting of visa restrictions in different regions, as practice shows, unfortunately always have to be dealt with by member states themselves at a later stage. i think this is a matter of security policy, not some gift that we can just hand over to the applicant countries. that is why i think it is necessary to ask all countries for security guarantees before they give their citizens a green light to enter the european union without a visa.
i too welcome the fact that the question of the visa-free regime for taiwan has been put on the agenda of this parliament. i think we're paying off our debt. similarly important partners of the european union have been granted and continue to enjoy visa-free travel. taiwan is the fourth most important trading partner of the european union in asia and is approximately as important to taiwan and the european union in the return journey. we expect that the visa waiver will further strengthen these already very close relations. we know that there is close cooperation not only in economic and commercial fields, but also in research, technological development, education and culture, and with visa-free travel we can always expect that these areas will also be strengthened. the condition we have set for the european union to have visa-free travel for all 27 member states is going to happen this year, so cyprus, bulgaria and romania will also be granted visa-free travel, and i think that's very important. i also want to emphasize that the changes in taiwan in the last 50-60 years are very important. taiwan has built a democratic institutional system and has gone from a poor country to an extremely strong economic country, so we certainly don't have to worry about, as many have mentioned, any pressure from illegal immigration that this visa-free regime would bring. we can have a lot more positive expectations about this.
in writing. my colleagues and i yesterday adopted by an overwhelming majority the resolution tabled by mrs maria da graça carvalho on simplifying the rules on the financing of the research framework programmes. the resolution is a step in the right direction, but more decisive action is needed. our current regulatory system requires a degree of bureaucratic preparedness that excludes it initiatives, smes and smaller research institutions from the pool of actors that can attract resources. so instead of the best researchers getting the grants, the best accountants get the grants. financial control is currently unable to manage almost any risk, which is unsustainable when it comes to investments in research and development, often with unknown outcomes. and risk should be managed, not excluded. there are appropriate regulatory solutions for this, and they must be strictly adhered to. but regulations that are structurally incompatible with the nature of research need to be reformed. another equally serious shortcoming in the allocation of fp7 funds is geographical asymmetry. researchers from the new member states have little chance of winning grants compared to those from the old member states. as long as the european union's research framework programme aims to give all of europe's talents the opportunity to excel, it cannot afford such a distinction in its implementation.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen of the house of representatives, my colleagues! the european court of auditors has done a good job in preparing its report on the audit of the union's budget for 2009 and the work of mr caldeira and the members of the european court of auditors should be acknowledged. i agree with those who say that the report clearly and unequivocally shows that the trend in the use of eu funds is improving and the error rate is decreasing overall. at the same time, we must draw clear and unequivocal conclusions from the report as to what we have to do here in parliament, in the commission and in the other institutions of the union. the reduction in the error rate means nothing less than that the european court of auditors says that the use of billions of euros of eu funds may give rise to suspicion of wrongdoing, irregularity or even criminal activity. all of which means action. i would like to emphasise that i consider it extremely important that in the coming period the european court of auditors should show, by country, the extent to which the country is in error in the use of eu funds and the quality of the control mechanism in that country. this can help to further reduce the error rate.

the crisis in the livestock sector has many causes. those who say that one of the reasons for this is unfair competition caused by imports and those who talk about the dangers of the mercosur negotiations are right. i'd like to talk about the rise in grain prices. it was foreseeable that cereal prices would rise, as i have said many times here in the european parliament. the european commission has done nothing about it. one of the reasons is the dismantling of the intervention, which was a serious mistake. it should be noted that the world grain trade is concentrated in fewer and fewer companies. this is also the case in many member states of the european union. the european union has given up on having a safety net, which will only increase speculation in the future. fluctuating cereal prices cause losses not only to cereal producers but also to livestock farmers and consumers. but beyond that, the cost of this is borne by european taxpayers. remember that the european union sold its 2004-2005 intervention stocks at a huge profit. cereal prices have been low in recent times. if the european union had intervened then, they could now sell the grain at huge profits, helping the livestock farmers too. but we don't have those stocks, and that's not only bad for producers and consumers, but it also deprives the european union of helping hungry countries like pakistan.

mr president, i wonder whether the house would agree that, constitutionally speaking, there is another interpretation of the whole idea of separation of powers and checks and balances? in the international arena and on the european political scene, we cannot simply transfer the national model of separation of powers and checks and balances. the way i see it – and i wonder whether colleagues would agree with me – is that, in a system of separation of powers and checks and balances, the council actually represents nation states, and whatever remains after the lisbon treaty of the sovereignty and independence of nation states. it acts on behalf of nations, whose governments are elected. in european democracies, governments are elected via a democratic, electoral system. on behalf of their nations and their peoples, they exercise a check and counterbalance the power of parliament. would the house not agree that there is another interpretation of checks and balances?

in writing. i welcome the fact that the european commission is launching a substantial work programme for the coming year. today, we see rapidly changing economic, financial, and international political conditions override our plans almost week by week. that is why i think it is important to review the commission's work programme several times and to update it next year. i am pleased that the document identifies job creation as one of the key tasks for 2011, but i am missing the employment and social legislative initiatives. the commission has finally decided to develop a framework strategy for the roma in europe, but i think it is necessary to accompany this with legislative proposals aimed at inclusion and non-discrimination. when the barroso commission was set up, it promised that its proposals would always be preceded by a social impact assessment, but i saw no sign of this in the work plan that was drawn up. i expect the commission to be ready to receive and manage citizens' initiatives signed by one million eu citizens from the beginning of 2011. in the face of the open and covert attacks on media freedom and fundamental democratic rights in the member states, i consider it particularly important that the eu body, as guardian of the treaties, pay greater attention than ever to the independence of information, the system of democratic checks and balances, government decisions that threaten the rule of law and, if necessary, take measures to protect fundamental democratic values.

on behalf of the ppe group. - officer, officer, please. although we are accustomed to treating the proposals with a critical but helpful attitude, in keeping with the tradition of this parliament, i must now say that i welcome and support the idea that has just been put forward to the fullest extent. i am particularly pleased that the commissioner has used the definition of full employment as the objective of those who can and want to have the means and opportunity to work. i support all four of the main priorities, better functioning labour markets, better quality jobs, more effective employment policies and better working conditions. even if we see that all this will not be easy to achieve. we agree with what you said about flexicurity, although i have to say, it's really time to talk about the details. we use it a lot and we think in many ways about what it really means. we believe that it is necessary to respond to changing working conditions in line with the principles of better regulation. it is therefore appropriate to bring the working time directive back into the spotlight, although i note that we do not consider a new directive to be necessary for the posting directive, but we are ready to discuss the implementation and the experience gained from it, and if necessary, to amend it where appropriate. i would like to draw attention to the need to regulate atypical forms of work. this means a whole new set of employer-employee relations in terms of social security and other conditions, and there are obviously many gaps in this area. we need to increase mobility, we need to pay more attention to the cooperation between public employment services and finally, mr president, i can see that time is running out, and i fully agree that in some ways low qualifications cost more than the crisis. so we need to get everyone to think about proper training, flexible training, recognizing the possibility of formal and informal training.

the european union is rather unpopular among hungarian farmers, especially small farmers. not only because we have had to sacrifice 100% of our markets in favour of foreign food imports in return for a fraction of the agricultural subsidies that were due to the old farmers in the member states, but also because, as the subject of our sitting today, excessive bureaucracy and those terrible administrative burdens are really - as has been said here on several occasions - hindering hungarian farmers in what is their real mission: farming. let me mention that in the old member states, hungary for example, there is another serious complicating factor, namely the state socialist or communist tradition, which leads some of the state officials to believe that they are not for the citizen and, where appropriate, for the farmer, but on the contrary, the citizen is for the state and the bureaucrat is for the state official. such public officials are burdening even beyond the existing rules, absolutely unnecessarily, humiliating and abusive, by regulating the farmers, making their lives difficult. we're getting complaints all the time. the latter concerned, for example, the refusal to pay the costs of an event organised by means of a call for tenders under the second pillar, on the grounds that the host had failed to obtain three quotes for the wholesale outlet he had provided to the participants. it's a classic case of humiliation, of excessive order, of domination. what's the cure? if such abuses can be complained about not only by the decision-maker, because we know that this is a death sentence attempt, but by the european union itself, once we have become members, and once the european union has paid these subsidies, the european union should create a control mechanism to which one can turn and where such humiliations, abuses are investigated and sanctioned.
in writing. the commission communication peace in the 2020 perspective contains few specifics on the practice of reducing administrative burdens. the word 'simplification' also occurs only by default in the text in relation to cross-compliance rules. however, the cap is too complex in other areas, often not transparent or understandable to farmers. one of the most important tasks would be to eliminate double regulation. several member states have imposed stricter national requirements than those required by eu law. this in itself distorts competition in the internal market. it would be appropriate for the different cross-compliance authorities in the member states and their departments to coordinate in order to avoid the same operator being subject to different checks within a short period of time. in the light of the current state of telecommunications and information technology, the establishment of a common database would make this possible. in the commission's reform proposal, environmental aid remains in pillar ii, but at the same time this green flag has been incorporated into the criteria for direct payments under pillar i. how this reference is made is not known today. i'd appreciate it if this positive step didn't bring another administrative burden.
mrs lulling asked us to support the motion for a resolution. and we can do that with a good heart, because we're going to have a really great document. the most important statement of this document is that it calls on the european commission to ensure that, after 2013, the existing support schemes that exist in the beekeeping sector are maintained and strengthened. it is in dire need of these subsidies because products produced by beekeeping are not recognised by the market in a way that would ensure the survival of the beekeeping sector. and beekeepers provide a lot of pollination services for agriculture and biodiversity conservation. a very important statement of this document is that it calls on the european commission to change the rules on the labelling of honey products as part of the quality policy. the current rules allow for abuse by labelling as honey of european origin honey which actually contains only a very small amount of european honey, in fact the vast majority of it, possibly even of inferior quality imported from third countries. and it is also very important to note that this document calls on the european commission to submit a legislative proposal to prevent the labelling of products containing less than 50% of sugar from honey as honey products. i have been fighting for these issues for years here in the european parliament. we have not yet succeeded in integrating it so perfectly into any single document as we have done in this motion for a resolution. i hope that the european commission will take up parliament's recommendations and find a way to solve these longstanding issues in the next six months, within the framework of quality policy.
i would also like to thank the rapporteur and mrs lulling for their role in this report on the state of beekeeping, and if i may, i would also like to thank the world-renowned hungarian beekeepers for sharing their knowledge and experience with me and for allowing me to pass on all the information we received from them to the committee. when the hungarian beekeepers look at the finished document, they will see your invisible signature on it. what i am very much lacking, what was not accepted in my amendments, is the financial recognition of the public good effect of beekeeping. now, beekeepers produce honey, beeswax, propolis, pollen, but that's just a small part of what they do. the big part of it is that they're multiplying the value of pollination, both qualitatively and quantitatively, for other agricultural crops. it's a public good that should be honored somehow. we have recommended the introduction of the concept of community beekeeping and, even if this has not been achieved at present, we are certainly proposing that beekeepers be given financial recognition for their work for the public good at member state level.

mr president, members of the council. i'm pleased to see that the importance of systemic pension reforms has been recognized at the meeting. however, they do not provide equal opportunities for all actors within the framework of the stability and growth pact. hungary also raises the issue of discrimination and has called on the union to take into account the cost of pension reforms in the calculation of government deficits. in my view, unless you want to discriminate against countries with freedom of choice, the contributions to private pension funds should be taken into account in the budget deficit. the matter is urgent. the good news is that the peace settlement is now open for discussion at the december board meeting. i ask you to take a non-discriminatory political decision and to forward it to the commission as soon as possible so that the legislative process can begin as soon as possible and then be concluded in a way that reassures everyone.
in writing. i am pleased to see that europe is increasingly committed to the single energy market. this is illustrated by the fact that both today's report by mrs kolarska-bobinska and the european commission's latest energy strategy and infrastructure priorities call for structural change. it is finally becoming clear to all of us that for breakthrough success we need significant investment, the removal of administrative barriers and concrete action plans. this is the only way to make the main objectives of the lisbon treaty - the single market, security of supply, energy efficiency and the spread of renewables - a reality for all member states. i consider it a great step forward that the abovementioned documents treat energy development in central and eastern europe as a top priority. the gas crisis of last year made it clear to western countries that the member states of our region are highly dependent on one source of gas and that the single market does not work here. europe has recognised that access to alternative gas sources, the construction of the north-south gas corridor and the elimination of isolated markets will increase regional security of supply and make a significant step towards the single market. in parallel, the strengthening of the electricity networks and the regional interconnection of oil pipelines will also broaden the scope of our region. however, the commitment cannot be expressed at the level of strategy-making. i am confident that structural changes will be made as soon as possible in order to achieve the single market effectively. rapid and transparent authorisation is achieved, regional initiatives are developed and new funding instruments are made available.
in preparation for the cancún climate conference, the european union and its member states should finally recognise that we cannot influence the united states, china, india and the emerging major economies to fight climate change by setting new and new unilateral targets. in this light, the 40% unilateral co2 reduction commitment proposed by the greens seems excessive. a 30% unilateral commitment makes sense only if it conceals real action. i agree with mrs hedegaard that the union can regain its leading role if it produces concrete results in terms of investments in research and development, the creation of green jobs, the construction of the smart energy grid and green growth.
in writing. at the press conference of the people's party yesterday, you must have heard about the so-called hfc-23 scandal. i myself find it extremely outrageous that european taxpayers should pay almost 70 times the price for the destruction of a greenhouse gas produced by the production of a refrigerant gas that was already subject to a ban, and that this should greatly increase the extra profits of some companies, mainly chinese and indian. it is scandalous that the european commission, citing investor confidence, does not plan to immediately suspend trading in these allowances and change this unsustainable situation before 2013. this is all the more scandalous in the light of the commission's efforts at international climate negotiations to prevent the newly acceded member states from retaining their national kyoto quotas. this is despite the fact that the quotas for the former socialist countries, including hungary, are based on actual emission reductions, for which the eastern european countries have paid a heavy price since the changeover. the scandal that was revealed yesterday is that although european consumers have paid 1.5 billion euros to date to get rid of hfc-23, the concentration of this gas in the atmosphere, which is 12 000 times more harmful than carbon dioxide, has increased significantly over the last two decades. it is therefore not surprising, ladies and gentlemen, that in the face of such double standards, cancun will not have a unified eu position, as in copenhagen.

the ombudsman report makes it clear that the lack of transparency in the eu institutions is one of the biggest problems. most of the complaints are about the commission, which, by its repeated delays in responding, is shaking confidence in the european institutions. increase transparency by optimising the work of the institutions, by broadening communication, while ensuring that costs do not increase. the european union has already spent a lot on propaganda, on its own advertising. moreover, the number of complaints is likely to increase further with the introduction of the lisbon treaty and the incomplete or delayed application of european legislation in the member states. in order to prevent this, extended and coordinated judicial training for national judges, legal practitioners, public administrations and public officials should be organised. citizens should also be made aware that they can challenge an act infringing eu law before national courts. in the first half of 2011, hungary will hold the rotating presidency of the european union, and therefore we, the hungarian representatives, have a particularly important task in implementing legal harmonisation.
in writing. in the margin of the annual report on the ombudsman's activities, may i, as parliament's draftsman of opinion on public access to documents, refer to institutional transparency. i was pleased to see that the author of the evaluation, mrs nedelcheva, paid particular attention to the links between transparency and good administration when examining complaints from citizens to the ombudsman. the right to good administration has become mandatory under the lisbon treaty, but the full exercise of personal rights is equally important. in one of our recent meetings with mr diamandouros, we discussed the delicate balance between institutional transparency and data protection. we also highlighted the june judgment of the european court of justice in the bavarian lager case, which brought transparency issues to a head, and is of particular importance because it provides guidance on cases where an alleged or real public interest conflicts with the right to the protection of personal data. i was very pleased to see that mr diamandouros also stressed the principle that personal data should only be disclosed with the consent of the data subject. i therefore consider it important to emphasise that i strongly support efforts to make the work of the eu institutions more transparent and to establish appropriate administrative techniques. the european ombudsman's work is focused on promoting this, according to the annual report.

in writing. the issue of maintaining visa-free travel is not a technical issue but a clearly political one, but visa-free travel is always based on mutual trust, mutual commitments. and the message for the countries concerned in our debate today is that, having won the visa waiver, they have not stopped the long line of work that comes with the commitment to provide their citizens with information about what visa waiver means, so that the opportunity is not abused. the visa waiver granted to serbia and montenegro provides access to the european perspective, in particular to young people, who are the building blocks of the europe of the future. the exemption granted two years ago was an important political message for these countries, and breaking it could have very serious consequences. it is equally important to maintain visa-free travel for hungarians of the future, for citizens who live on one side of the border or the other, speak the same language, and have close family and cultural ties. for countries rushing towards the union, the creation of conditions for living side by side without borders is of paramount importance.

the following item is the joint debate on the following reports:- report by petru constantin luhan on behalf of the committee on regional development on territorial, social and economic cohesion (a7-0309/2010), and- report by ramona nicole mănescu on behalf of the committee on regional development on good governance and eu regional policy (a7-0280/2010)
mr president, i would like to thank you for your service. my fellow members of parliament! hungary is in a unique position when it comes to establishing and supporting crossborder economic entity regions. it is a historical fact that these regions once formed a unity in the carpathian basin, therefore jobbik representatives support and consider necessary cooperation between the regions in the current situation, thus also blurring the temporary borders erected between the hungarians. however, instead of direct funds, channelled and managed through brussels, financial resources can only be envisaged in the form of projects initiated and approved by the countries concerned in partnership and joint cooperation. at local, regional level, both the identification and the management of problems can be done more effectively, taking into account the cross-border nature of the economically connected regions mentioned as an example, and this would be encouraged even if only economic aspects were taken into account. similarly, simplifying rules, involving smes in european projects and reaching out to economically less developed regions would all be welcome if these popular measures did not lead to an expansion of the powers and control of brussels and the commission by relegating national governance to the background. with the emphasis on the regions and their direct economic support, a new era may begin for the hungarians of the carpathian basin.
in writing. - my fellow members of parliament! i would like to congratulate mr luhan on his work on this report. i would like to draw your attention to two points in connection with this report. in the context of the link between the implementation of the eu 2020 strategy and the improvement of competitiveness, it is important to stress that closure and the improvement of competitiveness are mutually reinforcing processes. we cannot concentrate on supporting the most developed regions in order to maximise the union's competitiveness, as this threatens to further fragment the less developed regions, leading to significant social tensions and instability throughout the union. it is also important to note that, while cohesion policy makes a substantial contribution to the implementation of the eu 2020 strategy, it cannot be responsible for achieving the objectives of the strategy on its own. therefore, coherence between the eu 2020 strategy and the achievement of the objectives of cohesion policy should be ensured and other policy areas should contribute to the achievement of the objectives of the strategy to an appropriate extent. as a second comment, i would like to underline that i fully agree with the rapporteur that gdp should remain the measure of member states' entitlement, because gdp is the most reliable indicator of development currently known. at member state level, national authorities may use other indicators for the allocation of resources, but at union level gdp should be retained as the eligibility criterion.
mr president, i would like to thank you for your service. my fellow members of parliament! first of all, i'd like to thank the reporter. in my book, we're talking about one of the most important issues of the last century. my only heartache is that it happened at this late hour. as the report points out, the most vulnerable to advertising are children and adolescents, whose free will is still weak and who can be easily influenced. this applies not only to irregular, false and aggressive advertising, but to all advertising in general in a new world where they are confronted at an early age with the compulsive forces of integration into the consumer society. children's programming should be explicitly prohibited from advertising. and i should also point out that for young people, perhaps the most dangerous media outlet is not the traditional media, like tv or radio, but the internet. advertising here is the most aggressive, the most likely to hit the targeted consumer. the most audacious companies are not even concerned with personal data, but through social networks, they use it to penetrate the intimate sphere of the person. this must be prohibited and sanctioned. we must all propose action in the european union in this regard.

the rapporteur. - mr president, colleagues and members of the house! the adoption of the treaty of lisbon brought with it a vision and a concept of a strong europe in terms of community competences. and to the citizens, it promises to bring that vision to life. this is also true for the protection of fundamental rights, and unlike previous years, this year's report looks at these new legal bases, the strengthening and new licences and institutions, and the situation that has emerged. the report aims to take a comprehensive, innovative approach to the protection of fundamental rights. since december 2009, the protection of fundamental human rights has been based on several pillars in the union. on the one hand, it is based on the treaty of lisbon and the charter of fundamental rights, which has been given legal force. the latter has become the guiding principle for the creation and application of eu law, whether by the eu institutions or by the member states when they apply eu law. the european union's legal system will have a similar pillar when it accedes to the european convention on human rights, which is currently being negotiated, but the system of human rights guarantees in the member states' own legal systems will remain a similar pillar. these legal bases are also reinforced by the community institutions that have been set up, both at community and member state level. at community level, the commission, the fundamental rights commissioner's portfolio, the council working party in the council, the parliament and, i would mention among the agencies, the role of the fundamental rights agency. my report aims to draw attention to the need for these existing institutions and mechanisms to operate efficiently and transparently. whether it's decision-making, monitoring legislation, data collection or even information sharing, not prioritizing some rights over others. it is important that the institutions not only talk, but also react and reflect on each other. they use and build on each other's work in the field of human rights protection, making decisions consistently, objectively, and based on facts. in this report, we have therefore listed what parliament expects from these institutions. we reflected on the commission's communication of october, which followed a similar line of thinking. the report's fundamental objective is to emphasise that the starting point and the end point of the newly developed framework of fundamental rights must be the individual, that is, the citizen. this means providing citizens with the right information so that they know and are aware of their options and can take advantage of them. the system of protection of fundamental rights should be understandable, accessible and usable, since the whole structure is worth what the citizens of the union can make of it. in order to achieve these objectives, those institutions and the member states should play a balancing role in order to enhance the union's credibility in the eyes of its citizens. finally, my report focuses on areas where rapid action is needed, but also on medium and long-term strategies, such as roma integration, the fight against child poverty, the use of languages by minority communities, to name just a few. i hope that with the report adopted tomorrow we can send a clear and easily understandable message to the institutions mentioned: the commission, the council, the agencies, the national bodies, the courts, to make this system even more effective. i ask for your support tomorrow to ensure that in the post-lisbon period - the funds and institutions are in place - we act in the spirit of the treaty.
as a hungarian and a female politician, i am proud that a hungarian representative, gál kinga, has created a very important work on the better enforcement of human rights and put it on the table of the european union, and i thank her for this. what i'm missing is the emphasis on traditional civil and political freedoms, and we hungarians are hardly in a better position than anyone else in the union to emphasize that the european union has a problem not just with discrimination against minorities or roma integration when we talk about human rights, but with freedom of expression, freedom of assembly, freedom of speech, and we have no better proof of this than the situation in hungary between 2002 and 2010. the hungarian parliament's human rights committee recently adopted a very thorough and detailed report that, supported by court judgments, ombudsman reports and other means, proved that between 2002 and 2010, the hungarian socialist governments maintained their power by gross and continuous trampling on human rights. my question to the rapporteur is whether, in the present situation, this particular hungarian report can finally be put on the table of the european union and will the european union examine it, because that would make it really real that there is a change in the field of human rights here, or how can the 2006 mass shootings in hungary be avoided in the future if we can talk about the possibility of increased enforcement of human rights in the european union, especially given that the vice-president of the libe committee is still a person who was a member of the government that carried out the mass shooting?
mr. president, the officer's wife. the lisbon treaty also made the charter of fundamental rights binding and provided for the possibility of initiating infringement proceedings in the event of its breach. the social tensions resulting from the crisis have led to a reduction in democratic rights in some member states, rather than a move in this direction. restrictions on media freedom, restrictions on the right to strike, the avoidance and narrowing of social dialogue, the intensification of discrimination, the undermining of the foundations of the rule of law, legal certainty. as the guardian of the treaties, the commission has a duty to monitor these signals. we consider it important to establish the portfolio of the commissioner for fundamental rights and ask the commissioner to monitor the member states regularly and to speak up if fundamental rights, the basic principles of democracy, are violated, so that they are not only binding on the candidate countries before accession, but also afterwards. this also undermines the union's credibility.
the forward-looking part of the report has been highlighted before. finally, a report that summarizes the various human rights issues without elevating one area over the other. i am particularly pleased that the report makes a special point of non-discrimination and equal opportunities. the report is based on the charter of fundamental rights. the most important part of my speech is about that. the charter can only be successfully upheld and enforced if european society recognises that all people are equal and that equal treatment is the only way to achieve this. if anyone is disadvantaged in any way, through no fault of his own, then he should be helped by appropriate help, proportionately, to become an equal human being, an equal citizen. the road to that is very difficult and takes a lot of work. the report of galinga is an important step on the way to that. thank you very much for doing such a great job.

as a lawyer, i have been working for more than ten years on cases of victims of violence against women and children - domestic violence, rape, trafficking in women - and i have experienced that the social distancing measure, which is the subject of our session today, is one of the important and indispensable means of protecting victims, but it is not the only one. it takes a complex set of measures, legal counselling, psychological support, counselling sessions with the children involved, and it's very important that we listen to the victims, who are the most - i'm sorry to say - the experts in what they need. i have two questions for the commissioner and the reporters. one is how much they involve the victims of violence against women in this work, to share their experiences. the second is that the convention on violence against women, which is currently being prepared by the council of europe, the european union's twin, is well known to the commissioner and the rapporteurs and we are involved in preparing this work.
madam president, this report was certainly a major step along the road to fighting trafficking in human beings in general and trafficking in women in particular. the next major step should be to explore the crystal clear relationship and connection between trafficking in women and prostitution. on the one hand, we rightly talk about trafficking in women as a brutal, terrible crime that we should fight. on the other hand, we talk about prostitution as ‘sex work’, as a service. we talk about pimps and brothel-keepers as entrepreneurs, and we normalise prostitution. what i want to say is that the client who goes to a brothel, or the client who asks for the services of a prostitute, has basically no way of knowing whether she is a ‘normal’ prostitute – if that even exists since it is another matter whether there is ever consent to prostitution – or a trafficked woman. there is therefore no way of really attacking trafficking without attacking the sex industry.
in writing. – trafficking in human beings is a crime against human dignity that ruins the life of many children and women throughout the world. the current proposal is a breakthrough because it puts victims first and tackles the problem in a complex, cross-border manner. these new measures will facilitate the reconnaissance and prosecution of trafficking cases and will encourage victims to cooperate with the authorities. this will be binding legislation, subject to state enforcement. this type of regulation is the only one that can make a difference for all vulnerable people who might fall prey to trafficking. i also call on member states to follow the proposal in this text and punish those who knowingly accept the services of a victim of trafficking. fighting demand is key to fighting the roots of trafficking. this report is an excellent proposal, morally and professionally, and i congratulate my distinguished colleagues bauer and hedh for this. i am particularly happy that the council and femm and libe committees managed to create a text that is backed by all relevant institutions in the eu. i hope that it will soon be formally adopted and transposed into national law. the victims are eager to see it.
in writing. – as a result of the harmful tendencies affecting roma communities – such as low levels of education, deep poverty, marginalisation and discrimination – roma women and children are extremely vulnerable to trafficking. this modern-day slavery is deeply rooted in poverty and exclusion and, therefore, the elimination of the unacceptable living conditions that many roma face must be a major strategic objective. furthermore, the eu and the authorities of the member states must take much more vigorous action to combat this phenomenon, particularly regarding the eradication of organised crime and the protection of groups at risk, namely women and minors of minority origin. this initiative aims to adopt a broader concept of what should be considered trafficking and i believe that exploitation for begging, including the use of a trafficked dependent person for begging, regardless of his or her relationship with the perpetrators, must fall within this scope. penalties should be more severe when the offence is committed against persons vulnerable on grounds of age, gender, pregnancy, health conditions, or disability, and must pay due account of cases where the victim has been subjected to torture, the forced administration of drugs or medication, rape or other serious forms of psychological, physical and sexual violence.

mr president, mr commissioner, i have to ask you to leave. respected reporter! my fellow members of the house of representatives! i am very pleased that mr gauzès has produced such a fine report. this is a very important subject. the habitus and functioning of credit rating agencies is always relevant. the rapporteur's aspiration to monitor credit rating agencies and their products in an integrated manner according to uniform eu standards should be supported, but it is important to know exactly what and how they are rated. these questions need to be asked, because it is well known that before the crisis, these credit rating agencies gave excellent ratings to trillions of dollars of dubious or poorly backed securities, unfortunately giving unlimited room for speculation. first, the question of independence must be examined. if it is true that banks and certain investment groups finance the fees for the ratings, then the question arises whether the decisions of the credit rating agencies are made in a disinterested manner. the second question is the methodology of the rating. third, that coordinated downgrades and negative ratings can make any country insolvent, and thus ensure that it remains an increasingly vulnerable customer in the debtor market, forcing it to take on new loans. small, open economies and non-euro area member states are particularly at risk. i therefore believe that the acceptance that credit rating agencies are not responsible for the ratings they issue is incorrect. my fellow members of the house of representatives! so let's treat ratings appropriately, and only rely on them to the extent that they are credible, and let's use them as a guide in regulatory design. the report is more than supportive. the global economic crisis has shown the need to regulate and supervise the functioning of these institutions at union level.
