the following is the report by mr bendt bendtsen, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy, on the review of the energy efficiency action plan (2010/2107 (ini)) (a7-0331/2010).
mr president, i would like to thank you for your service. my fellow members of parliament! we believe that the climate change that is increasingly affecting us today is not caused solely by human pollution. but it is our collective responsibility, both locally and globally, to curb the destruction of our environment and to try to restore it to its original, healthy state in whatever way we can. accordingly, the promotion of energy efficiency is also welcome. our buildings are responsible for about 40% of the world's energy consumption, so addressing this is of paramount importance. we need to move towards zero-carbon new buildings and address the challenges of improving the energy efficiency of existing buildings. in addition, we need to continue to reduce vehicle consumption and emissions by promoting the uptake of hybrid and pure electric technology. i believe that we, as representatives, as politicians, need to set an example in energy efficiency.
in writing. - my fellow members of parliament! the review of the 2006 energy efficiency action plan is an increasingly pressing task, as the energy efficiency targets are the most lagging behind of the eu's 20-20-20 targets. many initiatives are already moving in the right direction, but current results are dwarfed by untapped potential. europe was just beginning to recognise its backwardness and to realise that it could not afford the luxury of neglecting energy efficiency. these investments can reduce emissions more cheaply than any other solution and immediately boost the economy. the positive development is that, following the decision of the european parliament, the unused resources of the economic recovery programme can be used for energy efficiency improvements and green investments. the energy 2020 strategy published by the european commission in november also identified energy efficiency as a top priority. the bendtsen report now makes specific recommendations on how to exploit the opportunities more effectively. i am pleased that innovative solutions such as smart grids and escos implementing energy saving investments have been included in the recommendation. however, the most important issue remains financing. energy efficiency grants should also be included in the future long-term eu budget, with particular regard to the building reconstruction programmes of the former socialist countries. this is an area where even with relatively little spending, spectacular savings can be made.
in writing. it is in the vital interest of the union and of all its member states to get as much energy as possible from as little energy as possible this can reduce external energy dependence, increase energy security and increase the union's competitiveness. energy efficiency should therefore be a top priority for the implementation of the europe 2020 strategy, and the principle should be made conditional on the call for energy tenders being made as widely as possible. the national action plan for hungary envisages very serious steps (from 2005 onwards) in both renewable energy and energy efficiency, in line with the objectives set in the europe 2020 strategy. the energy efficiency loan fund (eef) has been operating since 1991 to support energy efficiency investments by citizens. the hungarian government is also planning to improve the energy efficiency of the sector's largest consumers by 60% by modernising public institutions (installation of solar panels, insulation and modernisation of heating systems) on the basis of the draft comprehensive building energy and climate protection programme. these are fine plans, but their implementation has been seriously jeopardised by the financial and economic crisis that continues to this day. the commission should therefore pay close attention to national action plans and their implementation and to the transposition of existing eu directives into national legislation, which could be crucial for the implementation of the 2020 energy strategy and the achievement of co2 reduction targets. moreover, progress in this area can only be expected if the appropriate financial instruments are used and the legal framework is established.

rapporteur. − madam president, the european citizens’ initiative is a unique opportunity. it is the first time that european citizens can get together and let us know, with one strong voice, if they think that we, the european decision-makers, are not doing our job properly or missing out important issues. we badly need this. in a time when only 42% of the people who are entitled to vote in european elections take the time to actually vote, every new european instrument – especially an instrument like the eci – is of great importance. however, we realise that the high expectations that surround the eci may lead to disappointment and frustration. we can avoid this with a regulation that provides for its safe and efficient use, and we can help to bridge the famous gap between ourselves and european citizens. i would like to welcome the citizens here as well. mr lamassoure, ms wallis, mr häfner and i were all conscious of the huge responsibility on our shoulders. many points were raised during the discussions and we had to base our work on everyone’s strong sense of compromise. we were lucky to have good partners in this who showed reasonable flexibility and had the same goal as parliament’s rapporteurs, namely to reach an agreement as soon as possible, while maintaining the quality of our work and making sure that one year after the lisbon treaty we have a regulation for the most citizen-based provision of the treaty. i would like to thank commissioner šefčovič and the belgian presidency, particularly mr chastel, and their teams. we should also not forget the spanish presidency who contributed to advancing the dossier right at the beginning of the process. today we are presenting to you a compromise text which is supported by the commission and has also been adopted by the coreper. if we decide to support it and the council adopts it – which we hope – the regulation can enter into force on 1 january and be applicable 12 months later in 2012. we are pleased to say that the parliament’s key demands were accepted in the discussions. the admissibility check on an initiative will be based on clear criteria at the point of registration, not after many signatures have already been collected. to ensure the initiatives are well founded and have a european dimension, a citizens’ committee of at least seven members from seven member states should be set up to register an initiative. apart from reducing the risk of unserious initiatives, the citizens’ committee offers incontestable advantages for organisers as well. if the initiators of an eci are disorganised at the beginning of the process, they have a network and have many language versions of their initiative. they would have significantly less difficulty in collecting one million signatures. it is clear that, even though the requirement for the citizens’ committee initially seems to be a constraint, it would in reality streamline the rest of the procedure. the commission will help organisers of an initiative by providing a user-friendly guide and setting up a point of contact. if an initiative manages to collect one million signatures, a proper follow-up will be guaranteed, including a public hearing at parliament. the review of the regulation is of crucial importance as this tool is the first of its kind. as per our suggestions, it is extremely useful with regard to the difficult issue of how to verify the signatures. it is up to member states to do this, but we urged them to demand as little personal information as possible. member states will have some flexibility in choosing what information is required in each country, but i am glad to say that many of them are going for less data than originally planned. we believe that the regulation reflected in the compromise text is citizen-friendly and does not create cumbersome obligations for, and frustration amongst, the organisers.
the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has delivered an opinion. - as a member of the libe committee, i would like to join those who congratulated the rapporteurs and would like to thank the work of the commission and also the work of mr šefčovič and mr chastel in the council, who have contributed very much to the creation of a good, acceptable and really moving towards simplicity text, which i think will have very strong support. there was a lot of debate and a lot of good suggestions were put on the table. and the many good suggestions also meant that we had to combine all of these things to really make the final proposal as user-friendly as possible. getting a million signatures is a very big achievement, a very big job. we want to be able to reach out to those who can't contribute online but want to express their opinions on paper. we'd love to hear your feedback, and we'd love to fix any problems that arise on the ride as soon as possible. europe will surely be much, much richer with this tool.
madam president, the launching of the citizens’ initiative is unquestionably one of the most important developments in the process of european integration. it constitutes a qualitative redistribution of power within the european union and has the potential to generate significant innovation in european politics. the placing of citizens at the same level as parliament and council, by offering citizens the right to call on the commission to bring legislation forward, has far-reaching implications. for one, the new institution creates space to question one of the fundamental features of the way in which the entire integration process has been designed – namely that, from the outset, it has been elite-led. in many respects, this has been very successful but there is equally little doubt now that, without the active support of the citizens, the integration process runs into obstacles. indeed, there is growing evidence that citizens see the eu as remote, complex and impenetrable. they have had very little reason to engage with the eu because the means of doing so have been absent. this is what the citizens’ initiative will remedy. it sets up an instrument that has the capacity to bridge the gap between the eu and the demos. it is in everyone’s interest that the instrument should now work and should work as effectively as possible.
rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to thank my colleagues for all of their remarks. i am very glad that the majority among us is favourable to adding a new layer to our european democracy. it is completely natural and just to be expected that there are fears, but if we try our hand at something completely new we cannot answer all questions and calm all fears beforehand. however, i am confident that the regulation that we present today tackles many of the challenges that we could imagine during the preparation of the regulation. we should not be afraid of this new facet of democracy. we shall listen more clearly to european citizens’ voices, and we should not be afraid to do so. this is one of the long-term goals, is it not? we should only be afraid that the eci is used not in the spirit of the treaty, not for its intended purpose but abusively or not at all. we have done our best to reduce this risk in the regulation with the help of sylvia kaufmann, who made the first report on the eci in this parliament. i would also like to thank maroš šefčovič’s and alain lamassoure’s whole teams and our other colleagues. i would also to thank the team working for the hungarian presidency of the european union for their cooperation. i would like to underline that the implementation of the regulation is crucially important and, to this end, i would like to offer my help and cooperation to the incoming hungarian presidency. i am confident that the vote in an hour will support the compromise reached between the european institutions. this is the only way that we can give a nice christmas present to european citizens: a solid regulation on the european citizens’ initiative. let us not make them wait any longer. thank you very much for your attention and i would like to wish you, and all of the citizens here who bore witness to this, a merry christmas and a happy new year.
in writing. it is very important for citizens to have the opportunity as soon as possible to take initiatives in a predetermined way, within an appropriate framework, on the issues that most affect their lives. as you know, i am the head of the national association of the deaf and hard of hearing, one of the oldest organizations in hungary representing people with disabilities. based on my experience here, i can already say that the results of mr lamassoure and mrs gurmai's work will be tangible: citizens' initiatives. i am particularly disappointed that the million4disability initiative, launched in 2007 by the 80 million disabled people in the european union, which produced 1.35 million valid signatures, has failed to produce results. now there would be consequences, results, and i thank everyone for the work that has gone into this.
mr president, i just want to say that we are withdrawing amendment 14.
we all know that the financial crisis has led to the growing appeal of public pension schemes in many european countries, which offer greater security. many countries are reviewing their systems and are trying to strengthen the public system. although pension systems are fundamentally a matter of national competence, it is important to see where we are going in europe in these issues. i therefore welcome the fact that point 30 of the decision on the commission's work programme, which has just been adopted with the support of the three largest political groups in the european parliament, the people's party, the socialists and the liberals, stresses the need to strengthen the first, the public pension bill. my country, hungary, just yesterday took an important step in this right direction with a law passed yesterday. the debate on pensions in the union on the white and then the green papers should be conducted in this spirit, and this is what the european parliament is calling on the european commission to do, and i am therefore very pleased with this proposal.

in writing. at a meeting of nato ministers in october 2008, john craddock, the head of nato's european forces, stated: russia's invasion of georgia has prompted nato to review its assumptions about the security of its member states. now, after the lisbon summit, is that statement still valid? the agreement with russia is a political move that can serve to consolidate our security. but it inevitably raises serious ethical and strategic questions. is nato prepared to renounce, in the interests of cooperation, the representation and defence of the democratic ideals on which the euro-atlantic alliance is based, which in many respects are in sharp contrast to the russian concept of democracy? perhaps it is unnecessary to remind you of the intimidation and harassment of people, the unexplained circumstances of the death of journalists and lawyers in russia. or it would be difficult to dismiss as a non-provocative gesture the recent nomination of wikileaks founder assange by russia for the nobel peace prize. will the prospects of prospective members be adversely affected if russia looks with disdain on their prospective nato membership? the question arises: do nato leaders take into account the vulnerability of certain member countries and their still open, pending issues with russia? the historical trend towards reconciliation and agreement is certainly welcome. at the same time, we must be clear about nato's historic responsibility to be a great power that is legitimate and loyal to our common european values, as a result of the agreement on the missile defence programme, to which it says a firm no.
rapporteur. − mr president, it is only about one year since the entry into force of the lisbon treaty, which envisages considerable changes in the delegation of powers by the european parliament and council to the commission. a few months ago in this parliament, we discussed my report about delegated acts. delegated acts are when parliament and council delegate powers, but from now on, compared to the previous situation, we can take them back any time. we can set a deadline, we can change and we can veto if the commission is not in line with what the legislator wanted. in the case of the present regulation we are discussing based on article 291 of the treaty, we are speaking about implementing acts. in the case of implementing acts, the situation is a bit different. here, it is the member states which exercise their rights on how to implement the different legislation of the european union. so there is a clear difference. the new regulation and the new system of delegated acts gives us very wide powers. from parliament’s point of view, it changes our influence considerably, especially in certain areas of legislation. however, in the field of implementation, we have the right as parliament, as legislator, to regulate how the implementation procedure for the member state should go. i think parliament was right after long negotiations to take the position that we would like to confirm and get clear commitments from both the council and the commission that parliament’s rights are met. after very long negotiations, we have made very serious progress in this area which has influence and an effect not only – and particularly not only – in the area of implementing acts, but also on delegated acts. even in the case of implementing acts, parliament got and kept the right of scrutiny which means that, on the basis of the agreement with the other institutions, we have a procedure which we can apply. my first priority was that we could keep these important positions. there is also another issue here, which is that there are other ongoing discussions in this parliament represented by other committees – especially the committee on international trade, the committee on development and the committee on foreign affairs – and also between parliament and council on the legislation concerning financial instruments and whether and how they should be regulated. my goal in this framework was that this regulation should not prejudge this discussion. we want to help our colleagues who are fighting our position in an important discussion with the council so that we can finally end up with the cooperation of the council. finally, since my time is over, i would like to thank both the commission and the council for their flexibility in the negotiations. i understand that it was very difficult to get an agreement in council. i think this is the deal which parliament wanted and this deal can be satisfying for all members, especially because this is not only about the text we are discussing now, but we expect both the commission and council to take part in the common understanding concerning these important issues. we also expect declarations which make clear that there is a clear commitment from the commission for the alignment of the remaining part of the acquis communautaire. this text attached to my report should be satisfying for all members of this parliament.
rapporteur. − mr president, i should like to thank everyone for their contributions, both now and during the last nine months we have been working on this issue. first, a procedural thing. many colleagues have voiced their worries about the fact that we are rushing into a first-reading agreement under pressure from the council. both the commission and council and several colleagues involved in the negotiations can testify that the pressure was the opposite. it was parliament and myself who said to the council that if it did not reach agreement then the whole thing could fall apart. we got a very clear commitment from both the other institutions because like parliament, as mr šefčovič just mentioned, they are for the stability of the lisbon treaty. the later we accept, the later we adopt this kind of regulation, the longer the old comitology procedure – which we do not like because of its many problems – will go on. from that point of view, nine months is not a short time and could encompass two readings. there has been full transparency, and several colleagues can bear witness to that. i have been in the conference of committee chairs six times during these nine months and have also met with shadows and coordinators from all the committees. it was i who decided to go for agreement because we were getting everything i wanted. maybe the council and the commission do not like it, but we got everything. what does a rapporteur do when everything i and the different committees requested is there, although perhaps not in the form we want? mr de castro is right. his requests are not in the form of the text of this regulation, but this is about implementing acts, and they are there in the commission’s commitment. i have done this alignment – the omnibus – before, and the same commitment was given by the commission, which it honoured with very minor exceptions. in that sense, i thought i could trust this promise and if the commission does not honour it, i will stand up and claim it and align, as mr de castro is doing. in that sense, there is no pressure. the pressure came from me. through good cooperation in many difficult negotiations we agreed on something which i can unreservedly offer to the house to take on board. thank you very much to everybody, especially to the colleagues who took part in this very difficult process. i apologise to the general public, which does not understand a word of what we are discussing, but i can definitely say that transparency and parliamentary control of european union issues will be much better after this regulation is adopted.
in writing. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your support. i can fully agree with the report's finding that there is a huge gap between the citizens of the union and those of the european member states, as more than half of them do not have a positive view of the union. by renaming the title 'european heritage' to 'european union heritage' and making amendments, we are launching yet another grandiose, costly and unnecessary eu self-promotion and propaganda campaign. apart from the trifles of naming the slovak nationalist and separatist monument to stefanik as an exemplary legacy of european union integration, this new initiative will not bring people closer to the union. it is also not clear why the title 'european heritage', if it does not want to duplicate the existing unesco world heritage list, wants to extend it to third countries and which countries does it mean?
the debate so far has made it clear that there is a serious conflict of interest between those who have made the switch to the new system and those who have not yet done so. but there is also a serious contradiction between the old and the new member states. i fully agree with mr kalinowski that the old member states had much more time to make the transition and even before accession they handed over the old, outdated structures to the new member states. however, it is clear that the rules must be respected and that the european union is again putting its own producers at a competitive disadvantage by imposing stricter standards on its own producers than those imposed on external suppliers for imports. not only does it require it, but it often fails to control it effectively. so i think that the european commission will now take another decision that puts eu producers at a disadvantage vis-à-vis external markets.
in writing. − i consider european values to be important, such as hungarian graffiti, which must be recognised and rewarded. in this case, however, the tradition of actually awarding the prize to assets located in the union should be maintained. it is unacceptable to extend this to third countries, because it would only double the unesco world heritage list. i am also confident that this prize will bring a return to traditional european values and that it will be a real recognition to win this title. i abstained from this vote because of the contradictions in the report.

mr. president, i have to go. my fellow members of parliament! i of course deeply condemn the way eritrean refugees are being treated and i of course support the european union taking steps to resolve the situation of eritrean refugees as soon as possible. but the direction that this report is taking is simply a dead end. two points i find quite amazing. to create a financial fund to help these refugees, that's perfectly acceptable to me, given that millions of people in the european union are living in insecurity and poverty. in hungary, 3 million people are destitute, most of the pensioners cannot afford their medicines, and this is a typical trend in europe, so first of all we should help the people of europe, in addition to, of course, looking at the refugees with empathy. the other is that member states adopt it on a voluntary basis. well, they should realize that the european union is saturated with immigrants, so the european union should mediate between eritrea and the eritrean refugees so that they can return home safely.
